# Gaps archive: closed-row table (moved verbatim from `docs/history/bug-hunts/gaps-archive.md` lines 1-379)

# Chezzi — gap backlog, ARCHIVE (closed rows + every bug-hunt session log; the LIVE ledger is `docs/contributing/gaps.md`)

Catch-all backlog of missing / shallow surface. **Not a commitment** — draw from it when a feature
earns its own milestone. Categories: **bugs** (fix, don't backlog), **root causes** (one change that
unblocks many gaps), **language**, **stdlib**, **IO/runtime**, **tooling/ecosystem**, **deps**.

**Audit history:** first stdlib pass 2026-07-07. **Full four-axis audit 2026-07-14** (IO/runtime,
stdlib breadth, language features, tooling) — that pass found one live data-corruption **bug**, three
cross-cutting **root causes** that were each recorded as unrelated footnotes, and a whole missing
**tooling** category. It also found the file's own #1 entry ("number format-specs") had been **shipped
and never de-staled**. Re-audit periodically: a gap backlog nobody re-reads rots into a to-do list for
work already done.

**External dogfood pass 2026-08-17 (`W8-1..W8-20`, the newest and the first NOT run by the people who
built this):** ten developers new to Chezzi, one area each, 343 Chezzi programs checked against 45
reference programs in Python/Go/Rust. It is disjoint from waves 1–7 by construction — those hunted
soundness classes the authors already suspected; this one had no model of the implementation and so found
**silent wrong answers, scheduler defaults, message quality, and stale prose** instead (exactly one
soundness row in twenty). **All twenty were live while every standing gate was green**, which is the
pass's meta-finding — see `## Session log — 2026-08-17`. Closed so far: `W8-18` (doc drift),
`W8-2` (the discarded-carrier warning, 2026-08-17, the latter along with the un-numbered **airlock
trap** section below it), `W8-14` (every runtime stack-trace frame now names its file, 2026-08-17),
`W8-15` (both the `check` and `test` `--errors=json` halves, 2026-08-18), `W8-5` (`json.parse`'s
and `json.stringify`'s depth aborts, 2026-08-18) — the last three all on
`feat/span-file-and-stdlib-contracts` — `W8-6` (`regex.replace_all` rejects Python group refs,
2026-08-27), and `W8-20` (`json.encode`, the `dumps`-shaped inverse of `decode[T]`, 2026-08-28,
TICKET-013). `W8-43` (the shared structural-depth budget across nested
protocol-hook re-entries, 2026-08-18) was found while working this backlog and is filed
already-closed.

**External dogfood pass, wave 2 — 2026-08-18 (`W8-23..W8-42`):** nine independent agents, again with no
model of the implementation, again judging every answer against Python / Go / Rust **run on the same
box**. 30 findings; **27 reproduced in-repo**, and they were filed only after all 15 then-open `W8-*`
rows were re-run and confirmed still live — which is what kept two of them from being filed twice
(they are the same defect as `W8-3` from a different axis, and are folded into that row). Two did NOT
reproduce and are recorded with their measurements rather than filed (a false-`deadlock` shape: 0
failures in 320 runs across two binaries and four worker counts; `io.flush` losing stdout: 0/220).
Four more were filed with their stated shape **corrected** by the re-run. It is disjoint from wave 1
the same way wave 1 is disjoint from waves 1–7: seven of its rows are P0, three of those destroy or
corrupt data (`W8-24` overwrites a user's file, `W8-23` and `W8-35` return confidently wrong numbers),
and **not one of the 30 was reachable by a standing gate** — see `## Session log — 2026-08-18`.

**Pre-freeze bug-hunt waves:** 1–5 (2026-07-11 → 07-13), then 2026-07-18, 07-20, 07-22, three on
07-23, and **wave 7 (2026-07-28)** — batch A swept the **host boundary** (the native/CLI seam where raw
OS bytes become Chezzi values): 3 findings, all FIXED, and it re-confirmed wave 6's meta-finding
(the panicking `std::env::args()` had three call sites, not the one the report named).
**Wave 6 (2026-07-25)** is the largest single haul (**19 findings**) and the first to sweep the two
surfaces the wave-5 residual named as never-audited (FFI: 4 defects; GC + new object layout: clean). As of
2026-07-28 all 19 + the 3 carve-outs are fixed, plus one follow-up found by adversarial review of the
W6-9 branch (**`W6-9b`**, the half-byte-exact parity oracle, fixed 2026-07-28); `W6-9r` (all four items,
CLOSED 2026-08-16), `W6-10s`, and `W6-10r` were the other disclosed residuals — all now CLOSED (`W6-10s`/
`W6-10r` 2026-08-06). Read its session log
before touching `io`/`process`/FFI/`RwShared`/module-snapshot code. Its meta-finding — **5 of 6 P0s are "a
fix applied to SOME arms of an N-way set"** — is the highest-yield remaining lever. **Wave 7
(2026-07-28)** is running against exactly that lever; `W7-3` (a `recover:` inside a cancelled task's
`defer` was bypassed) and `W7-4` (two sibling closures over one captured local got separate cells across
the airlock) were both instances of it and are **fixed** — session logs at the end of this file.

## TABLE — the whole backlog at a glance, **2 OPEN ROWS** (updated 2026-09-05)

> **Reading note (2026-08-17): the table stopped being empty.** It had zero open rows for exactly one
> day. An **external dogfood pass** (2026-08-17 — ten developers new to Chezzi, one area each, 343
> Chezzi programs against 45 reference programs in Python/Go/Rust, nothing taken on the docs' word)
> filed **W8-1..W8-20**. Three are closed: **W8-18** (the doc-drift sweep), **W8-2** (a discarded
> `Result`/`Option` now warns — and the *prescription* filed in its Fix column turned out to be wrong on
> its top-level half; read the row before re-implementing it), and **W8-14** (every runtime stack-trace
> frame that carries a bytecode span — the headline and every `at FUNC (called at …)` line — now
> renders `path:line:col`, so a stdlib fault like `flag.chz:132:19` no longer reads as an unattributed
> `line 132` in the user's own file. TICKET-048 (closed 2026-09-03) is the residual this left: a
> nursery-deadlock and an Executor-join-deadlock fault were still raised with `Span::RUNTIME` rather
> than a real span, so their headline still rendered `line 1, col 1` with no file — see the struck-through
> W8-14 row's note below.) **W8-21 and W8-22** are two LANGUAGE
> milestones decided on 2026-08-17 out of the W8-2/W8-19 discussion — success-coercion at `T?`/`T!E` sinks
> (CLOSED 2026-08-30, TICKET-025), and giving a caught `Error` its origin span (CLOSED 2026-08-30,
> TICKET-026) — not dogfood findings; they are design decisions with the
> ancestor survey and the rejected alternatives recorded in their rows. The rest are open and are
> listed FIRST below, before the historical struck-through rows. Read its session log
> (`## Session log — 2026-08-17`) before working any of them — several share one fix.
>
> The pass's shape is the finding above the findings: **the semantics are in good shape and the layer
> around them is not.** 40+ CPython differentials produced **one** differing byte (`NaN` casing), a
> 7,000-op `std.collections` fuzz found zero mismatches, and `defer`/`panic`/`recover` is byte-identical
> to Go — while five separate paths return a silently wrong answer, the scheduler's default is its worst
> setting, and the diagnostics layer knows what is wrong and won't say what to do about it. **None of
> W8-1..W8-17 was caught by the standing gates** (4375 Rust tests, 586 Chezzi tests at two worker
> counts, the CPython differential, conformance): every one of them is either a *silent* wrong answer
> the suite has no assertion for, a *performance* fact no test measures, a *message-quality* fact no
> test reads, or a *doc* claim no test executes. That is the lesson to carry into the freeze — a green
> suite measures the assertions you thought to write.

> **Reading note (2026-08-16).** The cooperative `--serial` engine was **removed** on this date
> (`docs/design/roadmap.md` §2b). Session logs below that measure it, compare `serial` against `M:N`, or call it
> "the parity oracle" are **dated records of what was true then** and are kept verbatim — that is the
> point of this file. Nothing below still describes a flag you can pass. Two rows that existed only
> because of that engine (**N10**, **N6g**) are struck; what closed each is recorded in its own row.

Every **W1–W7 / N / B** row below is struck through — that stretch of the table reached zero open items
on 2026-08-17 (the last one, `min`/`max` → `Option`). The **W8-\*** rows above them are the open set.
Struck rows are kept, not deleted, as a chronological record of what was found + how
it was fixed; a new finding gets a new row, not a resurrected old one. **No memory-unsafety is left
in the ledger** — W6-8, the
last one, was fixed 2026-07-27 — and as of 2026-08-04 **nothing left in the ledger aborts the host
process**: W7-11, the last one, was fixed that day (`:4771`). Anything NOT listed here is either fixed or a safe-direction
observation. **Wave 7 batch A (2026-07-28) adds no row** — its three host-boundary findings
(`W7-1`/`W7-6`/`W7-7`) all landed FIXED; see its session log. Its deliberately-deferred sibling —
the **lossy path DECODE** in `fs.list_dir`/`walk`/`glob`/`canonicalize` and `os.getcwd` — was filed as
**W7-8** and is **FIXED 2026-07-31** (the `PathLike` protocol + `path.Path` type; see its session-log
section). The lossy-byte family now has **no unswept member**: B1, R1, W6-4, W6-9, W6-14 and W7-8 are
all closed. (`argv`/`env` remain a deliberately lossy surface — see `docs/reference/stdlib.md`.) That sweep
covered what PROGRAMS emit; the same decode inside the **detectors** is its own sub-family and was
swept later — `W6-9b` (the parity oracle) and **`W7-30` 2026-08-07** (the CPython differential, which
outlives `--serial`). Both were byte-blind while every language surface around them was already
byte-exact, which is the standing lesson: *when a change widens what a program can emit, audit the
comparators in the same commit.*
**The Executor drain milestone (W7-5/W7-5b/W7-5c) is CLOSED 2026-08-03**: `W7-5` (run-all drain,
lowest-index-fault propagation) and `W7-5c` (every faulting task's output flushes) were fixed 2026-08-01
— see the W7-5 session-log section — and **`W7-5b` is FIXED 2026-08-03** by the eager-execution
milestone (`docs/design/roadmap.md` §2c). Note the correction: eager execution did *not* dissolve W7-5b by
deleting the queue (it kept one). It dissolved it by changing what the program-exit join walks — a
heap-independent registry of `ExecutorCore` `Arc`s shared with every worker, instead of the per-`Vm`
`Vec<GcRef>` that died with its task's heap. **`W7-5d` is FIXED 2026-08-05** — a dead stdout is no
longer a hard halt, so the run-all drain covers it like any other ordinary fault, and **`W7-5e` is
FIXED the same day** — the counter that gate reads is now bumped inside the one door to streamed
stdout, so a *VM* write it cannot see does not compile. All five filed W7-5 rows are closed. Note the
scope: this is the VM's own sink. Bytes that reach fd 1 WITHOUT going through it — FFI calling libc
`puts`/`write` — are counted nowhere and are filed separately as **W7-20**.
**Keep this table in sync when a section is retired** — the
reason it exists is that "which of these is still open?" previously required reading 1400 lines of
chronological log. The `gaps.md` column holds **approximate anchors, not exact line numbers**: every
later edit shifts them and nobody re-numbers the whole table (`W7-27`'s was already 166 lines off in
2026-08-07). Search for the `### <item>` heading; treat the cite as "roughly here".

| item | gaps.md | what | why it is still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| ~~**W8-1**~~ | `:236` | **String interpolation silently eats regex quantifiers, and the docs taught the form that breaks.** `p := "\\d{4}-\\d{2}"; print(p)` → `\d4-\d2`; CPython `print("\\d{4}-\\d{2}")` → `\d{4}-\d{2}`. Interpolation is always on, so `{4}` is a hole, not a quantifier — and the result is **still a valid regex** (a digit then a literal `4`), so `regex.find` returns `Ok(None)` for every line and *nothing anywhere reports a problem*. In the reporters' log-analyzer this surfaced as `lines=0 malformed=2010` and was the longest single debugging stretch of the campaign | **DOC HALF DONE 2026-08-17**, code half open. `docs/reference/syntax.md` §10 and `docs/reference/stdlib.md` §`std.regex` now both say *use `r"…"`; in a normal string `{n}` is interpolation* — the drift was that §`std.regex` taught the doubled-backslash normal-string form verbatim while the raw-string advice lived in §10, far from where you are when you write a pattern. Open: the optional **lexer nudge** — warn when an interpolation hole's contents are *entirely digits*, which is never a useful expression and is exactly this bug's signature. Cheap, and it converts a silent corruption into a compile-time nudge **FIXED 2026-08-29 (TICKET-018).** A hole whose whole text is ASCII digits now renders literally instead of interpolating, so `"\\d{4}-\\d{2}"` keeps its quantifiers. |
| ~~**W8-2**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-17 — a discarded `Result`/`Option` now warns; and the Fix column filed below it was MEASURED WRONG on its top-level half and deliberately not shipped.** As filed: `fn f(): g()` where `g() -> Result[int, Error]` returns `Err("E")` — `chezzi check` said *ok: no type errors*, `chezzi run` printed nothing and exited **0**, while the same `g()` at module top level aborted with `runtime error: unhandled error: E`, rc=1. **What shipped is a WARNING, not an error** — Rust's `unused_must_use` is the governing ancestor because both carriers are `#[must_use]` there and the drop is a *lint*, not a rejection. Measured on the release binary: *warning (line 4, col 5): the Result returned by 'g' is discarded — bind it* `r := g()` *, or discard it explicitly* `_ := g()` — program still runs, rc unchanged, and `check --errors=json` carries `"severity":"warning"` | **CLOSED, and the prescription is the part to read.** The Fix as filed said *"top level becomes a compile error"*, removing the asymmetry. **That is wrong where it would fire, and was not shipped.** Measured, same program, two spellings: bare `g()` at top level → `runtime error (line 3, col 1): unhandled error: E`, **rc=1**; with the escape the warning itself recommends, `_ := g()` → prints `after`, **rc=0**. The runtime already checks a top-level drop (`Op::PopExprStmt` → `top_level_error`, `src/vm/exec.rs`), so "is discarded" is a **factually false** claim in that position and obeying it *disables the only check the program had*. The rule is therefore gated **OFF at module top level** and fires only inside a non-top-level proto that can hold a statement — a `fn` body, a `spawn:` block, a `defer:` block — each measured swallowing at top level. The gate was derived from the RUNTIME, not from a plausible checker flag: an exhaustive walk of the **eight `FnComp::new` sites** in `src/compiler/mod.rs` (only `<toplevel>` sets `is_toplevel`); `if`/`for`/`while`/`match` arm/`parallel:`/`recover:`/`wait:` arm all compile into the *enclosing* proto and were each measured aborting at top level, so all stay silent. **The asymmetry is KEPT and justified, not removed.** Measured non-fires, each pinned by a `no_warn` test: `defer f.close()` and `spawn g()` (call forms — Go's canonical unchecked idioms, see **W7-46**), an inline-expr fn body and a value-block tail (implicit returns, not drops), `?` / `??` (they yield the unwrapped payload). `o()?.len()` **does** warn — optional chaining re-wraps to `int?`. A carrier laundered through a type parameter (`fn drop_it[T](x: T): x`) escapes the rule — measured silent, rc=0 — and **Rust is identical** (`T` carries no `#[must_use]`), so that is an ancestor-aligned limit recorded in `syntax.md` §9, not a residual. Corpus: 11 `_ :=` sites, all in `fn`/`test fn` bodies. Not `W7-48` (the `?`-in-`spawn:` swallow), a different site. **The W8-19 pressure behind this row is untouched and still open:** `Option`/`Result` still have **zero** methods, so every fallback is still a 3-line `match` — fixing W8-19 is what makes W8-2-shaped code rarer, the warning only makes it visible. Docs: `syntax.md` §9 (position table), `spec.md` entry model. **REVERSED 2026-09-02, TICKET-038 — the top-level gate above is deleted; `Op::PopExprStmt`'s top-level abort is gone, so this warning is now the ONLY check on a dropped carrier, and it fires in every position, top level included.** Measured before/after on `fn g() -> Result[int, str]: return Err("boom")` followed by a bare `g()`: was `runtime error (x.chz:3:1): unhandled error: boom`, rc=1; is now the W8-2 warning on stderr plus the following statement's output, rc=**0**. Reason: all three owning ancestors exit 0 on a dropped error value (Rust warns via `#[must_use]`, Go's compiler is silent, Python raises only on `raise`), and the old top-level net was already escaped by both `r := g()` and `_ := g()`, so any binding form defeated it — this warning's own advice (`_ := g()`) used to change the exit code, which is now fixed by removing the runtime check it contradicted. One narrowing accepted, not compensated: a value that only LOOKS like a carrier at run time — a user `enum` with an `Err` variant, shadowing the builtin — while its statement's DECLARED type is not a carrier (e.g. `fn f() -> int: x := Err(5)?; return x` then a bare `f()`) now exits 0 with **no diagnostic at all**, where the old runtime check aborted on it regardless of declared type. Accepted because the warning channel is type-driven by design: the checker has no static reason to look inside a non-carrier return type. |
| ~~**W8-3**~~ | `:236` | **`Shared.set()` inside `Shared.update()` loses the write with no signal, and the docs describe behaviour the runtime does not have.** `docs/reference/stdlib.md` said all reentrancy self-deadlocks. Measured — at the default worker count and again at `CHEZZI_THREADS=2`, identical both times (the reporters saw it at every count they tried) — it does **three** different things: `get` inside `update` **succeeds** returning the PRE-guard value (a stale read); `set` inside `update` is **silently lost, rc=0** (`Shared(10)` + nested `set(99)` under an `update(+1)` ends at **11**); `update` inside `update` / `write` inside `write` **hang forever with no deadlock report**. Same for the `RwShared` analogues; `write` nested in `read` is the one legal crossing (it persists) | Notable because the deadlock detector catches its **three other shapes in milliseconds** with better messages than Go's — this path slips past it, so the hang is the only self-reporting cell and it doesn't report either. **Two fixes, independent:** (a) the lost `set` should fault (`set on a box already write-locked by this task`) rather than be overwritten by the guard's return value — a wrong answer becoming a message; (b) the nested-guard hang should reach the deadlock verdict, which per **`parked-is-not-stuck`** means it must be built from what is *impossible* (this task holds the lock it is waiting on — that IS impossible, so it qualifies) and not from what looks idle. Doc corrected 2026-08-17 with the measured 3-row table. **WIDENED 2026-08-18 (dogfood wave 9) — two more shapes, same root cause, and the filed prescription is re-derived.** The root cause is documented as deliberate: `docs/reference/concurrency.md:598` says `read`/`write` *"copy the value out of the lock and **drop the guard before running the closure**"*, so `update` is not a critical section at all — while `:596`, two lines above, promises *"the whole `write` is serialised so concurrent writers can't lose each other's updates"*. **(a) CROSS-TASK:** `s := Shared(0)`; one task runs `s.update(slow)` (300 ms), another `s.set(100)` at +100 ms → **`s = 1`**, the `set` silently lost, identical at threads 1/2/4; the paired Go mutex program prints **100**. Replacing the sleep with 0.4 s of pure CPU still prints `1`, so it is not a park artifact. **This is where the filed fix (a) is WRONG**: faulting the lost `set` is right for the same-task shape and wrong here, where the clobbering `set` is on another task and a fault would be the **[[parked-is-not-stuck]]** mistake — it must **block**. **(b) CROSS-BOX `update`-in-`update`** (`a.update`'s closure calls `b.update`, and the mirror) **hangs forever** — rc=124 at 1/2/4 threads, no output, no deadlock report — and `docs/reference/concurrency.md:605` blesses that exact program: *"(Re-entering a **different** box, or a same-box `read`/`get`, is fine.)"*. **(c)** the same body as a `test fn` under `chezzi test --timeout=2000` also returns **rc=124** — the runner itself hangs — while the same flag correctly reports `1 timed out` for a spin loop, so a `Shared` lock wait is invisible to the only mechanism that exists for hangs and this shape hangs CI forever. **Ordering constraint that falls out of (b):** today the inversion cannot happen through `get` *because* the guard is dropped, so holding the guard makes (b) MORE reachable — the deadlock verdict in fix (b) must land in the SAME commit as the guard, or the fix converts a silent wrong answer into a new class of hang. Wave-9 session log: `## Session log — 2026-08-18` **CLOSED 2026-08-29 (TICKET-016).** A same-box `set`/`update`/`write` re-entry now faults (`deadlock: this task already holds the update guard on this Shared box`) instead of silently losing the nested write, measured `Shared(10)` + nested `set(99)` under `update(+1)` now `rc=1` with that message instead of `11, rc=0`. A cross-task `set` racing an `update` now **blocks and lands** (`s = 100`, matching Go), via a process-global wait-for-graph update guard (`Vm::acquire_update_guard`/`release_update_guard`) rather than the per-box `update_lock` deadlocking on re-entry; a genuine cross-box wait cycle (`a.update` calling `b.update` while the mirror runs) now faults with `deadlock: two or more tasks each hold a Shared/RwShared update guard the other is waiting for (a lock cycle)` instead of hanging at rc=124, while the identical program with no cycle (a completes before b needs it) still runs to `done`. `get`/`read` stay guard-free (still read the pre-guard value) and `write` nested in `read` still persists. `docs/reference/concurrency.md:596-605` and the `docs/reference/stdlib.md` reentrancy table are corrected to match. |
| ~~**W8-4**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-29 (TICKET-015) — mutations from a `sort_by`/`sort_by_key` callback now fault instead of vanishing.** As filed: `w := [3,1,2]`, a key fn that does `w.push(100)`, then `w.sort_by_key(bump)` gave `[1, 2, 3]`, rc=0 — three pushes gone. CPython refuses: `ValueError: list modified during sort`. Same for a `pop()` in the callback | `Vm::sort_mutation_check` compares the live list to the rooted pre-sort snapshot by raw `Value` word immediately before the write-back, at all three sites (`list_sort_by`/`list_sort_by_key` in `src/vm/call.rs`, `list_sort_structs` in `src/vm/arith.rs`); a change now faults with `list modified during '<method>' -- a sort callback must not mutate the list being sorted`, matching CPython |
| ~~**W8-5**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-18 (task B1, `feat/span-file-and-stdlib-contracts`) — both sites fixed.** As filed: `json.parse` on deeply-nested input kills the process despite its `Result` contract. `"[".repeat(100000) + "]".repeat(100000)` matched with a correct `Ok`/`Err` `match` → `runtime error (line 43, col 9): maximum call depth (10000) exceeded`, rc=1, and the trailing `print("still alive")` **never ran**. Go returns `exceeded max depth` and CPython raises a catchable `RecursionError`; both stay alive. **Re-measured on the release binary, the identical repro** (task C, 2026-08-18): `err: exceeded max depth` then `still alive`, rc=0 — the `Result` contract is now total, no `recover:` needed | `std.json` is recursive-descent **in pure Chezzi**, so nesting depth in attacker-controlled input becomes recursion depth in the program — a caller that correctly matched `Ok`/`Err` used to die anyway, which made this a **crash/DoS on untrusted input**, not a cosmetic gap. **Fix: a `Parser.depth: int` counter, capped at `MAX_NEST_DEPTH = 2000`, returns `Err("exceeded max depth")`** instead of letting the VM's own 10 000-frame call-depth cap abort the process — 2.5× headroom below the measured death point (nesting depth 5000, no surrounding call frames). **SECOND SITE — `json.stringify` had the same defect and no error channel to fix it with — also CLOSED.** `stringify(j) -> str` can't return an `Err`, so it stays a **fault** past the same 2000-depth cap, but now named (`json.stringify: exceeded max depth`, via a `stringify_depth(j, depth)` inner helper) and recoverable under `recover:` — re-measured (task C): under `recover:` gives `Err`, message names itself, process stays alive; uncaught it still faults (rc=1) with a filed, named stack trace, the same shape CPython's `json.dumps` uses (`RecursionError`, itself only catchable via `try`/`except`, not silent). Both documented at `docs/reference/stdlib.md:1729-1748`. ~~`json.get` and `json.len` are self-recursive too (verified in source) and share the class.~~ **CORRECTION (task B1, 2026-08-18): false.** Both are flat single-`match` functions — neither calls itself; only `stringify` and the two `Parser` container arms ever recursed. Frames now name their file too (**W8-14**, closed the same branch). **Also found in task B1, not a bug: `stringify` has a SECOND, unrelated fault — a `Json.Num` holding NaN/±inf → `cannot serialize non-finite float to JSON`.** CPython's `json.dumps` emits a bare `NaN` (invalid JSON); Go's `encoding/json` returns `json: unsupported value: NaN`. Chezzi is right to refuse — a written contract (task B2), not a fix |
| ~~**W8-6**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-27 — `replace_all` now returns an `Err` naming `$N`.** **`regex.replace_all` with Python's `\1` silently emits literal backslashes.** `replace_all(r"(\d+)-(\d+)", "10-20", r"$2/$1")` → `Ok('20/10')`; the same call with `r"\2/\1"` → `Ok('\\2/\\1')`. No error, `Ok`, plausible-looking output. The dialect is **RE2 (the Rust `regex` crate)** and replacement is `$1`/`${name}`, but the docs named no dialect at all | **Filed as the SIXTH silent wrong answer, not a doc row** — same shape as **W8-1** (plausible value, rc=0, Python muscle memory, no diagnostic), and the two share one fix seam: validate the *replacement template* the way a pattern is validated. A `\<digit>` in a replacement string has no meaning in RE2, so rejecting it (or accepting it as an alias for `$<digit>`) is safe and total. Doc half done 2026-08-17: §`std.regex` now names RE2 and lists the four differences that bite — this one, the **reversed argument order** (`(pattern, subject, repl)` vs `re.sub(pattern, repl, subject)` — both `str`, so a swap type-checks), no lookaround/backrefs (each a clean `Err`), and `split` dropping capture groups Python keeps |
| ~~**W8-7**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-18 (`fix/mn-idle-policy-w8-8-w8-7`) — the default worker count is no longer the slowest setting; it is now at parity with the best one, and `sys` collapses to ~0.** As filed: **The default worker count is the SLOWEST setting.** Two CPU-bound tasks in one nursery, only `--threads` varying, 12-core Linux: T=1 **1.376 s** (sys 0.008) · T=2 1.396 · T=4 1.395 (sys 0.166) · T=8 1.455 (sys 0.663) · T=12 1.787 (sys 2.269) · **T=0 (default) 1.826 s (sys 2.491)**. Monotone in worker count, and the sys column says it plainly: **idle workers thrash rather than sleep**. Go's equivalent is 0.08 s at 0.00 sys at both `GOMAXPROCS=2` and `10`. Also measured on the reporters' 10-core darwin box: 4 tasks T=4 1.24 s vs T=10 2.75 s (sys 7.25); 8 tasks T=8 burns 15.45 s user vs 8.78 s at T=2. **Re-measured on the release binary after the fix**, `examples/primes_parallel.chz`, 12-core Linux (real/user/sys): T=1 8.936/17.053/0.117 → 15.301/15.327/0.236 · T=2 7.943/15.133/0.096 → 8.743/16.733/0.030 · T=4 5.742/17.670/0.384 → 5.594/16.867/0.008 · T=12 7.860/29.523/10.729 → 5.586/16.879/0.003 · **T=0 (default) 8.225/32.595/10.110 → 5.961/18.475/0.009**. The monotone-in-worker-count curve is gone and the `sys` column — the whole diagnosis — drops **10.1 s → 0.009 s** at the default. Full sweep plus the Go / Rust / CPython twin programs on the same box: `docs/contributing/benchmarks.md` §"W8-7 / W8-8 idle-worker-policy fix" | **CLOSED — and like `W8-2`, the Fix column filed above was MEASURED WRONG; read it before re-implementing anything here.** The filed direction — *"an idle worker must park on a condvar, not spin"* — describes a defect the engine did not have: idle workers **already** parked on an untimed `Condvar::wait` (`src/vm/mod.rs:2803`, `take_runnable`), and there was no spin and no parking threshold to tune. The cost was on the **wake** side: `MnSched::yield_fiber` called `cv.notify_all()` on **every reduction-budget preemption** (`CONTEXT_REDS = 4000` dispatched ops, per fiber), waking all `W-1` sleeping workers, each into an O(W) `try_steal` probe that found nothing and re-parked — **O(W²) mutex/futex churn per time slice**, which is why the symptom was monotone in worker count and lived in `sys`. **Fix: delete that one broadcast** (`dfac4e64`); the yielding worker loops straight back into `take_runnable`, so the requeued fiber always has a consumer. **One hang regression, found in review and fixed in `6e88b3e0`:** a worker that yields and then leaves via `self.demoted` does NOT loop back, and the replacement thread spun at demote time has typically already parked untimed — zero consumers, rc=124. Fixed by notifying at the **departure** instead (`sched.cv.notify_all()` before `mn_worker_loop`'s `self.demoted` return): once per demoted-thread exit rather than once per 4000 ops, so the sys-time win is untouched. **Go correction worth keeping, because it is the kind that gets re-broken:** go1.26.6's `goschedImpl` **does** call `wakep()` on every preemption (`runtime/proc.go:4341`), damped by a spinning-M check (`nmspinning != 0`) or a failed `CAS(0,1)` — but its last line is `schedule()` on the same M, so the preempted `g` already has a consumer. Go's wake is **recruitment**, not liveness. Chezzi's guarantee is therefore not *stronger* than Go's; what Chezzi gives up by not waking is the recruitment of an idle worker mid-slice, which is sound here because a preemption re-queues work that already had a runner rather than creating any. Tests: `vm::tests::mnsched_yield_fiber_reachable_by_other_worker_without_wake`, `vm::tests::w8_7_demoted_fiber_yield_after_demote_does_not_strand_replacement`, and the new integration target `tests/chezzi_threads_sys_time.rs` (`sys < 0.03 * user` at the default worker count; RED pre-fix at 0.089–0.106) |
| ~~**W8-8**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-18 (`fix/mn-idle-policy-w8-8-w8-7`) — `--threads=N` now means N CPU runners, at N=1 too.** As filed: **`--threads=1` runs TWO workers.** 8 CPU tasks: T=1 real 2.569 user 5.093 → **1.98 cores**, byte-identical to T=2 (2.689/5.326); T=3 real 1.852 user 5.508 → 2.98 cores. Single-burn baseline at T=1 is 0.589 s, so 8× the work took 4.4× the time. Go `GOMAXPROCS=1` really serializes (0.51 s vs 0.08 s at 10). Confirmed three independent ways by the reporters: pure CPU burns, FFI `sleep(1)` calls that the docs say pin their worker inline, and an 8-burn-vs-1-burn ratio. **Root cause NARROWED 2026-08-17 (measured, 8-task program, cores = user/real): T=1 → 1.99 · T=2 → 1.99 · T=3 → 2.96 · T=4 → 3.95 · T=6 → 5.90. So it is a FLOOR OF 2 at N=1, not an owner-plus-N off-by-one** — every `N >= 2` tracks `N` correctly and only `N=1` gets one extra runner. `vm::worker_count()` (`src/vm/mod.rs:384`) is **correct** — it returns `n.max(1)`, so `--threads=1` really yields 1 — and `vm::pool` sizes itself from it, so the extra runner comes from the nursery **enlist/owner** path in `src/vm/sched.rs`, not from pool sizing. That is where to look — **and that is exactly where it was.** **Root cause:** an outermost eager nursery's runner budget is `1 (drainer) + helpers + joiner`, sized for `max(N, 2)` slots, so at N=1 the inline joiner (`Vm::join_eager_nursery`/`abort_eager_nursery`) ran a full fiber loop **alongside** the unconditional raw `chezzi-eager` drainer — two CPU runners at a budget of one. **Fix: two named pure functions in `src/vm/sched.rs`** — `eager_helper_wids(n) = 2..n.max(2)` and `eager_joiner_runs_fibers(n) = n >= 2` — gating the joiner's `mn_worker_loop` in **all four** arms (`join_eager_nursery` and `abort_eager_nursery`, outermost *and* nested). The **nested** arm was a second site found in review: `activate_eager_nursery`'s third branch (`sched.rs:764`, `mn.is_none()` with an outer eager scope already open) is reached when a `parallel:` opens while another `parallel:` on the same thread is mid-body — e.g. `fn work(): parallel: …` **called** (not `spawn`ed) from a `parallel:` body. Measured, `--threads=1`, cores = user/real: flat 1.04 → 1.04 (unaffected), directly nested 1.98 → 1.04, three-deep 1.98 → 1.04, called-helper 1.98 → 1.04; ten reachable shapes were enumerated and all measure ~1.00 post-fix. On `examples/primes_parallel.chz` `--threads=1` goes **1.91 → 1.00 cores**, matching Go `GOMAXPROCS=1` (1.00) and Rust N=1 (0.99) on the same box (`docs/contributing/benchmarks.md`) | **Two consequences, and the second is why it ranks above W8-7.** (1) The knob for serializing a flaky concurrency repro **does not serialize**. (2) `docs/reference/concurrency.md` called `--threads=1` "the safe single-thread mode", `CLAUDE.md` repeated it, and several stdlib design rationales are justified as *"measured at `CHEZZI_THREADS=1`"* — e.g. the `connect`-in-`Executor` starvation argument (`W7-59`). **Every one of those measurements was taken two-wide.** Note the two-worker floor is also why `pmap` at T=1 looked deterministic. **BOTH consequences are now discharged.** (1) `--threads=1` really serializes, so it is again the knob for pinning a flaky concurrency repro. (2) **Every claim in the tree that rested on a two-wide `CHEZZI_THREADS=1` was re-derived on the genuinely 1-wide binary, 2026-08-18 — all nine came back CONFIRMED or UNCHANGED-BY-DESIGN; none was false.** The load-bearing one is `docs/reference/concurrency.md:1077-1080`'s N8 claim (the argument that made deleting `--serial` safe): re-ran 15×, **15/15 faulted in 4–6 ms, 0 hangs, 0 timeouts**. The `connect`/`accept`-in-`Executor` starvation argument (`W7-59`) is UNCHANGED-BY-DESIGN and the reason is structural: an eager `Executor` job runs on the process-wide `vm::pool`, sized straight off `worker_count()` (`src/vm/pool.rs:38-40`), while W8-8's floor was in the nursery **enlist/owner** path — so `--threads=1` gave that pool exactly one thread before the fix and still does (re-measured: `Err` in 0.006 s, rc=0, no hang). `docs/design/roadmap.md:418`'s nesting bound re-measured at **max 4 live OS threads** at depth 7 / 128 leaves (the original cited 3; no repro for it survives in the tree, so the constant differs by methodology — nowhere near the 130 the gate exists to prevent). The `tests/chz` suite is 617/617 at `CHEZZI_THREADS=1/2/4/8`/default. The `pmap`-looked-deterministic-at-T=1 note above is likewise now really deterministic |
| ~~**W8-9**~~ | `:236` | **`duration.parse` caps magnitude by DIGIT COUNT, not value, so the bound is unit-blind and the documented round-trip is broken.** `"100000000000h"` (12 digits, 3.6e17 ms) → `Ok`; `"2562047788015ms"` (13 digits, **2.5e12 ms** — five orders of magnitude smaller) → `Err("duration out of range")`. Mechanism at `std/duration.chz:142`: `if intpart.len() > 12: return Err(…)`, a bound sized for the worst unit (`h`, ×3 600 000) and applied to **every** unit. It also breaks a guarantee `docs/reference/stdlib.md` stated as total — *"Round-trips exactly (`parse(d.to_string())` ⇒ `d`)"*: `millis(i64::MAX).to_string()` is `"2562047788015h12m55.807s"`, which `Err`s, and so does the `i64::MIN` mirror. Go round-trips `time.Duration(math.MaxInt64)` fine | **The correct guard is value-relative: `intpart_value > i64_max / unit_ms`** — computed per unit, which is the same one comparison and removes both defects at once (the false reject and the broken round-trip). `docs/contributing/gaps.md:2617` recorded the 12-digit bound as *deliberate* but acknowledged neither the unit-blindness nor the round-trip break, which is why it survived: the note made the symptom look like a settled decision. Doc corrected 2026-08-17 with the measured numbers **CLOSED 2026-08-28 (TICKET-012).** The filed symptom framing was wrong: `"100000000000h"` is 3.6e17 ms and FITS i64 ms, so it correctly stays `Ok` — restoring a reject there would have been its own bug. | ~~**W8-10**~~ | `:236` | **FIXED 2026-08-27 (TICKET-003) — `chezzi run -- <args>` now forwards everything after `--` unchanged, in manifest mode and after a file path alike, and `--` itself is consumed.** Was: `chezzi run --dir logs` → `chezzi run: unknown flag '--dir'`; `chezzi run -- --dir logs` → `chezzi run: unknown flag '--'` — program args existed only *after* a file path and there was **no `--` terminator**. `go run . --dir x`, `python -m pkg --dir x` and `cargo run -- --dir x` all forward. This also fixed a second, previously unmeasured half: file-mode (`chezzi run file.chz --dir logs`) already forwarded args after the path, but did **not** strip a literal `--` (`chezzi run file.chz -- --dir logs` measured `['--', '--dir', 'logs']` before the fix) — same new arm covers both. Tests: `tests/module_root.rs::bare_run_forwards_args_after_double_dash` (manifest mode, the reported symptom) and `::file_run_strips_double_dash_terminator` (the file-mode half). `chezzi test` and the shared flag helper have the same `unknown flag '--'` shape and are **not** fixed by this — separate row needed if in scope. | **Two adjacent scaffold problems, both fixed 2026-08-17:** (a) `chezzi init` printed `chezzi run src/main.chz` as the alternative way to run — that command is a **silent no-op, exit 0**, because the file form runs top level only and never calls `main`; *a program that defines `fn main()`, produces no output, and exits 0 is the worst possible feedback*, so the banner now says so explicitly. (b) the banner advertised `entrypoint = "src.main"` while the manifest it had just written said `"src.main:main"` — the advertised form also runs nothing; the banner now matches the file. The workaround documented in `syntax.md` §9b is no longer needed and has been replaced with the fixed behavior. |

| ~~**W8-18**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-17 — the doc-drift sweep: twelve claims that each sent at least one developer down a wrong path.** Fixed in this commit. In descending cost: **(1)** `from M import X` was written as the named-import form in `syntax.md`, `stdlib.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and 16 `std/*.chz` headers — it is a **parse error** (`unexpected 'from' in expression`); the real form is `import X from M` (`grammar.bnf:113`), and 3 of 10 developers hit it. **(2)** Structs are **reference** values (Python's model) and `syntax.md` §7 said nothing — the only nearby sentence was §Maps' *"Keys are value types (Go model)"*, which points a Go reader the wrong way; the language really is two-model (keys/Set elements snapshot, everything else aliases, and the spawn airlock copies — three models), now stated. **(3)** §`std.regex` named no dialect: it is RE2, replacement is `$1`/`${name}`, args are `(pattern, subject, repl)` — reversed from `re.sub` — lookaround/backrefs `Err`, `split` drops groups, named groups are write-only. **(4)** *"Plain float formatting matches CPython `repr()`/`str()` exactly"* — `NaN` prints `NaN`, CPython `nan`, on **every** path (bare print, `str()`, interpolation, container element, `.2f`, `>8`); `inf`/`-inf` do agree. **This was the ONLY differing byte in 40+ CPython differentials.** **(5)** Tuple element access is `t.0` — documented in **no** file, found by guessing Rust syntax, and `t[0]` → `cannot index into (str, int)` with no pointer to it; `grammar.bnf:564` already had `<postfix> "DOT" "INT"` so this was prose-only, and the `<lvalue>` omission is correct (`t.0 = x` → `cannot assign to field '0'`). **(6)** `for` iterates a **snapshot of the spine** — growing a `Map` inside its own `for` is a silent no-visit here and a `RuntimeError` in CPython; reported independently by 2 developers, and `stdlib.md`'s snapshot note was scoped to callback methods, implying `for` was live. **(7)** FFI docs/examples are Linux-only (→ **W8-11**). **(8)** `net.listen`/`connect` signature lines said `-> Listener`/`-> Socket`; both return `Result` (measured `Ok(Listener(open))`). **(9)** §`std.datetime`'s *"matches Python `fromisoformat`"* (→ **W8-12**). **(10)** §`std.json` never said `decode[T]` has no `encode` inverse (→ **W8-20**). **(11)** `concurrency.md:231-236` showed `fn worker(): spawn helper()` as an **error** — it compiles and runs since implicit nurseries landed. **(12)** the §2 bytes table omitted `extend` and slicing, both of which work and are documented in `syntax.md` §2 — the two docs disagreed about the bytes surface | **CLOSED, and the process lesson is the point.** Not one of these twelve could fail a test: `cargo test` executes `grammar.bnf` and the goldens, but **no gate executes a prose claim**. Eight of the twelve were *stale* rather than never-written — they described behaviour that had since changed (`spawn` in a fn), or a guarantee that had since acquired an exception (float repr, the duration round-trip in **W8-9**, the `Shared` reentrancy table in **W8-3**) — which is exactly what CLAUDE.md's "docs are part of the change, not a follow-up" rule exists to prevent, and it did not hold. | ~~**W8-21**~~ | `:236` | **LANGUAGE MILESTONE (decided 2026-08-17, CLOSED 2026-08-30, TICKET-025) — implicit success-coercion at a declared `T?` / `T!E` sink.** The *syntax* already ships: `T?` = `Option[T]` and `T!E` = `Result[T,E]` both parse and run today (`Token::Bang`, `src/lexer/mod.rs:116`), and `fn f() -> int!MyE: return Err(MyE(1))` works. What is missing is the coercion: `fn f() -> int?: return 1` and `fn f() -> int!: return 1` are both `type error: expected Option[int]/Result[int], found int`. **Decided rule set — exactly two coercions, both on the SUCCESS side:** `T` → `T?` gives `Some(v)`; `T` → `T!E` gives `Ok(v)`. `None`, `Some`, `Ok` and every `Err` stay explicit. Payoff, measured at the only positions a declared-sink coercion can fire: **102 `return Ok(` + 50 `return Some(` = 152 sites** | **The rejected third rule is the whole design content, and the reason is measured, not aesthetic.** The original proposal included `E` → `T!E` (a bare error value becomes `Err(v)`). It cannot be added here because **`str` → `Error` is a GENERAL assignability rule in Chezzi, not an `Err(...)`-local one** — it is an intrinsic protocol grant at `src/checker/ty.rs:516`, `(Protocol("Error"), Str) => true`, and `Result[T]`'s omitted `E` defaults to that same `Error` existential (`ty.rs:343`, `:428`). Verified: `e: Error = "boom"` and `fn t(e: Error)` called as `t("boom")` both compile. **That one line is what creates the collision**, so the overlap is structural, not incidental. So at any `T!E` sink with `E = Error`, every `str` value satisfies **both** sides: `return "boom"` in a `str!` function is `Ok("boom")` or `Err(Error("boom"))` with nothing in the type to decide. That is not an edge case — **`Result[str]` appears 19 times and `str!` 15 times (34 sites), the second-most-common `Result` shape in the repo**, versus `Result[str,str]` at 1. Rule 3 buys **81 `return Err(` sites** and costs the entire ambiguity surface plus a priority table plus a rule whose behaviour depends on `T`. **The ancestor survey says nobody resolves this overlap — they all make it unrepresentable:** **Zig** has all three coercions but its error side is an *error set*, a distinct kind carrying **no payload**, so a `[]const u8` can never be an error (documented; no Zig toolchain on this box, not re-run); **Swift**/**Kotlin**/**C#** coerce to optional but put errors in a separate `throws`/exception channel absent from the return type; **Rust** refuses the coercion outright (measured: `error[E0308]: expected \`Result<String, String>\`, found \`String\``); **Go** disambiguates by **position**, not type (measured: `(string, error)` returns `"ok <nil>"` / `" boom"` with no ambiguity). **Zig buys rule 3 by giving up error payloads; Chezzi's `Error` has `.message()` and `E` is arbitrary (`Result[str,str]` and `Result[int,MyE]` both legal — verified), so that trade is already spent.** Success unmarked + failure marked is also the better read, and it is Go's `return nil, err` logic. **The replacement for rule 3 is the `!"..."` sugar** — `return !"cannot read {p}"` for `Err(Error(...))`: `str!` + `"boom"` is undecidable, `str!` + `!"boom"` is not, so the sigil supplies the disambiguation the type system cannot. That covers the 81 sites without the collision and is a real reason for a sigil rather than mere brevity (`Err` already accepts a bare `str`, so brevity alone would only save 4 chars). **Remaining rules that MUST be written before implementing — all of them rejections, which is the safety margin:** (a) `V` assignable to `Result[T,E]` already ⇒ no coercion; (b) `V` reaches `T` only via ANOTHER coercion (`int`→`float`) ⇒ reject, do not chain; (c) `T` (or `E`) is an **unresolved type parameter** `V` could unify with ⇒ reject — **4 corpus sites use a generic `E`**; (d) the **nesting rule**, which is not hypothetical: `Option[Option[…]]` appears **5** times and `Result[Option[…]]` **3** times, so `return None` into `Result[Option[int], Error]` needs a stated answer (`None` binds the OUTER, `Ok(None)` must be spelled), same for `Some(x)` into `Option[Option[T]]`; (e) **declared sinks only, never an inferred return** — letting a coercion decide an inference is the `inferred-return-uninferable-unknown` trap. **Cost to plan for is the lowering, not the checking.** Chezzi's one existing implicit coercion, int→float, needed **13 `Op::CoerceFloat` emit sites**, a transient `float_elem_hint`, and an entire `FloatAliases` table — because, in `src/compiler/mod.rs:360`'s own words, *"the compiler keys on the SYNTACTIC declared type, while the checker keys on the RESOLVED `Ty::Float` — without this table an alias-spelled `float` sink would check clean and lower with NO `Op::CoerceFloat`, leaving a runtime `Int` under a static `float`."* That is the **`checker-superset-of-compiler`** class, paid once, for a scalar→scalar widening. This one is the same shape at MORE sink positions (return, arg, field, container element, map value, tuple element, generic instantiation) with a **heap-allocating wrap**, and an alias-spelled `Option`/`Result` sink (`type Maybe = int?`) is the exact analogue of the `FloatAliases` hole. Test matrix = one failing-then-green case per rule above, the 34-site `str!` case first. **Do NOT bundle with `W8-22`** — different layer, and bundling means one revert kills both |
| ~~**W8-22**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-30 (TICKET-026) — a caught `Error` now carries its origin span via `e.line()`/`e.col()`/`e.file()`.** **The filed prescription (a NEW native struct replacing the `Err` payload) was measured wrong before implementation and was NOT built:** on this repo's own binary, `e == "boom"` is `true` and `"{r}"` renders `Err('...')` (repr-quoting applied to an `Obj::Str`), asserted by `tests/chz/spec/repr_test.chz:64` and `tests/chz/spec/cancel_defer_recover_test.chz:25` — any payload-type swap breaks both. **Shipped design instead: the payload stays `Obj::Str`; the origin rides in a `GcRef`-keyed side table on `Heap`** (`err_spans: FxHashMap<u32, Span>`), stamped at the three `recover:` boundaries (`src/vm/exec.rs`, two arms in `src/vm/stmt.rs`) and pruned in `Heap::sweep`'s free branch (the key is a REUSED slot index). `line()`/`col()`/`file()` are a checker-side special case on the bare `Ty::Protocol("Error", [])` receiver (`src/checker/expr.rs`) — NOT added to `ProtocolInfo::methods`, which would un-satisfy every struct error type that has only `message()`. An error with no recorded span (a user `Err("...")`, a struct error, anything crossing a task airlock) answers `None`, never the `recover:` site. Verified: `/tmp/w822.chz`'s caught `xs[9]` fault reports `Some(3)`/`Some(15)`/`Some('/tmp/w822.chz')`, matching the uncaught form's `runtime error (/tmp/w822.chz:3:15): ...` exactly. Original text below, unstruck for the ancestor survey and rejected alternatives:** a caught `Error` should carry the line/col it came from. Today it carries nothing but text, and there is a **live asymmetry**: a *panic* reports its location, the same fault caught as an `Err` does not. Measured on one program: an uncaught index fault prints `runtime error (line 3, col 11): index 9 out of bounds (len 1)` with frames, while `r := recover: [1][9]` then `e.message()` gives bare `index 9 out of bounds (len 1)` — no line, no col, no file. Worse for a propagated user error: `fn a() -> int!: return Err("boom")` reached through two `?` hops yields `boom` with no way to tell which `Err` site produced it | **The location ALREADY EXISTS and is thrown away at ONE line, and because `Error` is a PROTOCOL the fix is additive.** `RuntimeError` carries `pub span: Span` (`src/vm/mod.rs:61-67`); the `recover:` boundary discards it at `src/vm/exec.rs:1392` — literally `let msg = self.alloc_str(rte.message); let err = self.alloc_enum("Result", "Err", vec![msg]);`. **CORRECTION to this row's first draft, which claimed "`Error` IS a bare `str`" — that was wrong.** `Error` is `Ty::Protocol("Error", [])` (`src/checker/ty.rs:428`), satisfied **structurally** by anything with `message() -> str` — measured: a user `struct MyErr` with a `message` method assigns to an `Error` slot clean, while `e: Error = 5` is rejected. `str` satisfies it by an **intrinsic grant** at `src/checker/ty.rs:516` (`(Protocol("Error"), Str) => true`), which is the only reason a bare `str` works there; and `Result[T]`'s omitted `E` defaults to this existential (`ty.rs:343`). What is a bare `str` is not the type — it is the **`recover:` producer's payload**, one call site. **Consequence: the origin rides on a NEW native struct satisfying `Error` structurally** (`message()` + `where()`), which `recover:` allocates instead of a bare `str`. `str` keeps its intrinsic grant, user error structs are untouched, and **the 336 `Err("...")` sites do not move at all** — no `==` change, no display change for a user-constructed error. **Ancestors split evenly, so this is a genuine choice and not an ancestor mandate:** Python attaches a full traceback automatically (and it is the single most-praised debugging affordance it has); **Zig** has *error return traces*, but **debug builds only**; **Rust** carries no location — `anyhow`'s backtrace is opt-in via `RUST_BACKTRACE`; **Go** deliberately refuses, and the idiom is manual per-layer context (`fmt.Errorf("%w")`) — `pkg/errors` added traces and the community largely moved back. **Recommended shape, three decisions:** **(1) capture the CONSTRUCTION span only, not the propagation chain.** A Zig-style return trace costs an allocation per `?` — **271 `?` sites** — which is exactly why Zig gates it to debug builds. A single origin span is **compile-time known**, so the compiler can bake it in as a constant operand and the runtime capture cost is **zero**; only the object gets wider. **(2) do NOT change the default rendering** of the new struct. The only display that can move is a **recovered** `Err`'s payload — measured exposure: **37 `.chz` files use `recover:`** and **2 `examples/*.expected` goldens print an `Err(`** — so keep the new struct's `str()` byte-identical to the message it replaces, expose the origin through a new accessor (`e.where()`), and let the **top-level unhandled-error printer** use it, which is where it actually pays. **(3) it subsumes part of `W8-14`/`W8-15`** — same `Span.file` → filename plumbing those two need, so sequence it after them and reuse the resolver **that now exists** rather than building a second one: `Program::file_path`/`ModuleProto::file` (the `Span.file` → `PathBuf` lookup, landed with `W8-14`), `lexer::render_span(span, path)` (the one `path:line:col` rendering rule, also `W8-14`), and `RunError::files: Vec<(u32, PathBuf)>` (the table snapshotted from `Program::modules` at the run boundary — the shape `type_check`'s own `Vec<(u32, PathBuf)>` return mirrors for `W8-15`'s check half). The open question to settle first is narrower than the first draft implied: **what the new struct is called and whether `where()` returns a `str` or a structured value** — the protocol is already the seam, so no side table and no change to `Error` itself is needed. Second: whether a **user**-constructed `Err("...")` also gets an origin (it would need the same compile-time constant at the `Err(` call site) or whether only fault-derived errors carry one — the asymmetry is defensible but must be a stated rule, not an accident |
| **W8-19** | `:236` | **Missing affordances — each one makes correct code visibly longer than sloppy code.** Not bugs; ranked by keyboard cost. **`Option`/`Result` have ZERO methods** — no `unwrap_or`, no `is_ok`, no `is_some`; every fallback used to be a 3-line `match` (**this was the pressure behind W8-2**; `??` now covers the `Result` fallback too, see below). **Closures are single-expression** — every comparator or callback with a branch becomes a top-level named `fn` (three of them in one small app — the reporters' count, not re-measured here); Python has `sorted(key=)`, Go has full function-literal bodies. **No `len`/`sorted`/`zip`/`enumerate`/`sum`/`min`/`abs`/`repr` globals** (all measured `unknown name`) — Python muscle memory misfires on every one, and `reverse`/`reversed` are paired while `sort`/`sorted` are not, so a non-mutating sort can't sit in a pipe. **Tuples aren't `Hashable`** — the commonest Python composite-key idiom needs a `struct` plus a hand-written `hash`. ~~**No struct `copy`/`clone`**~~ — **LANDED 2026-08-30 (TICKET-030).** Every struct now has an intrinsic, SHALLOW `copy()`. A deep copy was deliberately NOT added: Python splits `copy.copy` from `copy.deepcopy` because a deep copy must answer for cycles, for substructure shared on purpose, and for a value (`Channel`, `Socket`, `Shared`) that cannot be duplicated at all. This sub-item is closed; the row stays OPEN for its remaining sub-items, so no counter moves. **`path.join` rejects `Path` + `str`** — every `fs`/`os` call returns `Path`, so the most natural filesystem line in the language is `list elements differ: Path vs str`, and the workaround people reach for is the lossy `.str()` the docs warn against. (**The report's "and it emits a double slash" does NOT reproduce** — measured, `path.join([p.str(), "c"])` gives `/a/b/c`, and a trailing-slash input `"/a/b/"` also gives `/a/b/c`; the `.str()` objection is lossiness only.) No `path.abs`/`path.rel` either, and `fs.canonicalize` requires existence — measured `Err('/nope/zz: No such file or directory (os error 2)')` — so a not-yet-created output path can't be absolutized. ~~**No unbuffered/rendezvous `Channel`**~~ — **CLOSED 2026-08-30 (TICKET-028).** `Channel[T](0)` is now the rendezvous channel (Go's `make(chan T)`): `send` blocks until a receiver is already waiting, `cap()` reports `0` (was: `Channel[T]()` unbounded and `Channel[T](0)` faulted `Channel capacity must be >= 0`). `Channel[T]()` stays unbounded — a deliberate, now-documented divergence from Go's rendezvous no-arg `make(chan T)` (`docs/reference/concurrency.md`, `docs/reference/stdlib.md`). **Can't race `accept()` against cancellation** — `Listener` isn't a `Channel`, so `wait:` rejects it (`a wait arm must recv from a channel`); graceful shutdown becomes a polling loop where Go's `ln.Close()` unblocks `Accept` at once. **Statement-only `recover:`** — `print(recover: 1/0)` is a parse error; must hoist to a local | **Ranked fix order by damage prevented per hour: ~~`Option`/`Result` methods first~~ — DECLINED 2026-09-02, see (3) below** (the filed argument was that it retires the W8-2 pressure and that `unwrap_or`/`is_ok`/`is_some`/`ok_or` are pure additions to an existing type), so the head of the order is now **multi-statement closures** (it retires the `t.0`-in-a-closure workaround too, and unblocks the comparator shape that pushed three named fns into one app), then the **global helpers**, then `path.join` accepting `PathLike`. Tuple `Hashable` is already documented as absent — at `docs/reference/stdlib.md:1454` and `docs/contributing/gaps.md:3706` (**the report's `stdlib.md:1399` / `gaps.md:3455` cites are both wrong — those lines are a `pmap` row and an unrelated by-handle note; corrected here**) — and is still near-blocking for dict-heavy porting. **The rendezvous `Channel` and the un-selectable `Listener` are the two that are Go-divergences rather than conveniences** — both deserve their own decision, not a backlog line. Per **`stdlib-gap-deferral-bar`**: none of these may be deferred on "nobody asked" — a reference-language idiom **is** the need — only on verified cost. **Two more named 2026-08-18 (wave 9), each filed with its own defect rather than here:** `not in` (`not x in xs` parses as `(not x) in xs` and type-errors — **W8-28**) and `,`/`_` grouping in a format spec (`{n:,}` is a check-time reject — **W8-42**); grouping was the most-reached-for missing affordance of that pass. **DECIDED 2026-08-29 (chezzijr), two sub-items settled so they are not re-filed:** **(1) the global helpers are DECLINED** — `len`/`sorted`/`zip`/`enumerate`/`sum`/`min`/`abs`/`repr` will NOT become global names, because CLAUDE.md's convention is explicit that a new builtin fn goes in its owning `std.*` module and that a global reserved name is a one-way ratchet that breaks every example and `grammar.bnf` if it is ever moved out. The method forms (`xs.len()`, `xs.sort()`) already cover the need; the residual cost is Python muscle memory hitting `unknown name`, which is accepted. Do not re-file this sub-item; re-open it only by changing the convention in CLAUDE.md first. **(2) multi-statement closures get their OWN milestone ticket**, not a slot in this bundle — they are a grammar change (lexer, parser, `grammar.bnf`, compiler) and the ranked fix order above now puts them first. **DECIDED 2026-09-02 (chezzijr), a third sub-item settled so it is not re-filed:** **(3) the `Option` half of `Option`/`Result` methods is DECLINED** - every Option affordance the row names already has a measured spelling, all measured on the debug binary at `edc8fe34` with `o: Option[int] = Some(3)` and `n: Option[int] = None`: `unwrap_or` IS `??` (`print(o ?? 0)` prints `3`, `print(n ?? 0)` prints `0`), `is_some` is `o != None` (prints `true`), `is_none` is `o == None` (prints `false` for `o`, `true` for `n`), and the general form is a `match o:` with `Some(v):` and `None:` arms (prints `3`). CPython, the owning ancestor for scripting feel, spells the last two the same way (`x is not None` / `x is None`), so the comparison form is ancestor-faithful, not a workaround. The method form stays absent - `o.unwrap_or(0)` is `type error (w819m.chz:2:7): type Option[int] has no method 'unwrap_or'` - and that is ACCEPTED, not a gap. Do not re-file this sub-item; re-open it only with a use these spellings measurably cannot cover. **The `Result` half CLOSED 2026-09-02 under TICKET-039.** `r ?? 0` now type-checks and discards the error: on `r: Result[int]`, it yields `0` on the `Err` path and the payload on the `Ok` path, matching Rust's `r.unwrap_or(0)`. The pre-fix measurement, quoted verbatim: `'??' applies to an Option, found Result[int] — a Result carries an error that must be handled: use a match with Ok/Err arms`. `?` is not that fallback: it propagates, and at module top level `v := f()?` type-checks and then ABORTS on `Err` (`runtime error (w819e.chz:4:6): unhandled error: boom`, rc=1) instead of substituting a default. The rest of this row — tuple `Hashable`, `path.join` on `PathLike`, the un-selectable `Listener`, statement-only `recover:`, and the multi-statement closures of (2) — stays open and unranked-against here; struct `copy` and the rendezvous `Channel` have landed (TICKET-030, TICKET-028), and the `Option`/`Result` methods of (3) are declined. |

| ~~**W8-25**~~ | `:236` | **A closure over a MODULE GLOBAL loses the global's value at the airlock — the same expression gives `3` at module scope and `300` inside a function.** Measured: `n := 1`; a `spawn:` sets `n = 100` then sends `fn(x: int) -> int: x * n` over a `Channel`; the receiver calls it with `3` → **3** at module scope, **300** with the identical body inside `fn main()`; Go and CPython both give **300**. A closure's reference to a module global is a *late global load resolved in whichever task calls it*, not a capture, so it is neither moved nor copied at the airlock — which contradicts `docs/reference/concurrency.md` §7 (*"the airlock lowers a closure by value everywhere"*) and the checker's own airlock warning text, and is a live cross-heap read of one cell from two heaps in a shared-nothing model. No warning, no fault, identical at every worker count | **Decide first, then fix**: is a module-global reference inside a closure a capture (copy it at the airlock, like every captured local) or a link to the defining heap (then it must be a diagnostic, not a silent divergence)? Today it is neither. Related but distinct from the closed **airlock trap** section (a `spawn:`-task write read after the join now warns) — that rule fires on a *local*; this shape has no local to name. Same family as **`b33-closures-as-data`** and **`airlock-sendability-architecture`**: any new cross-heap store has to go through `to_wire_crossable`, and a global load never does **CLOSED 2026-08-29 (TICKET-016).** The human decision was CAPTURE: a closure's reference to its home module's `let`-bound globals is now snapshot-copied at the airlock alongside its captures, the same way a captured local already was. `Proto::global_free` (`Compiler::fill_global_free`) names every free global slot the closure tree reads and never writes; `Obj::Closure.gsnap` carries the snapshot; `Op::GetGlobalSlot` consults it before falling back to a live read. Measured: the filed repro now prints `300` at module scope, matching the `fn main()` case and Go/CPython. A slot the closure itself writes stays a late load (`n := 1; fn f(x): n = n+x; return n` still gives `4`, not `103`), and a closure that never crosses keeps reading live (`n := 1; f := fn(): n; n = 5; print(f())` still gives `5`). Review caught one more shape after the first fix landed: a spawned task's OWN write to a module global must stay visible to a module-level closure it CALLS (never crossing a channel) — the per-task module-snapshot replay (`ensure_snapshot`/`fault_module`) must never freeze a `gsnap`, since the closure and the global move into the same task's copy together; fixed by a `WireMemo::module_replay` flag that suppresses the snapshot on that one replay path. `docs/reference/concurrency.md` §7 corrected to match. **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (TICKET-051).** The `Obj::Closure.gsnap` mechanism this row describes is replaced: a crossing closure now carries no private snapshot at all — the airlock installs its free globals directly into the RECEIVING task's own module copy (filtered by two per-view bit vectors, `ModuleData.assigned`/`.carried`), so a closure's read and the task's own read of the same slot can never disagree. That closed five more measured divergences from Go/CPython, including a receiving task's own write losing to an arriving closure's stale value and a forwarded closure (A→B→C) missing B's own write. **REVERSED 2026-09-19 (TICKET-137, owner decision D2).** The install is deleted: a closure or generator reads the module globals of the task that RUNS it, so this row's module-scope case now prints `3` BY RULE (Go/CPython `300`); the migration path is a `Shared` (`docs/reference/concurrency.md` §7). |
| ~~**W8-26**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-27 (TICKET-001) — the entry source is read ONCE; `run` and `check` on a pipe execute the piped program.** As filed: `echo 'print("HELLO")' \| chezzi run /dev/stdin` → no output, **rc=0**; `echo 'x: int = "type error"' \| chezzi run /dev/stdin` → no output, **rc=0**; `echo 'x: int = "bad"' \| chezzi check /dev/stdin` → **`ok: no type errors`**. (c) **Neither `??` nor `?.` appears in `docs/reference/syntax.md` §4's table at all**, though that table's own note calls itself *"the contract for the Pratt parser"* and says *"the relative order follows Python"* | The `not in` half is loud (two type errors, not a wrong value) so it is an affordance + message problem: add `not in` as an operator, or teach `not` to say *"did you mean `not (x in xs)`?"* (**W8-17**'s Levenshtein/hint layer). Re-measured: the same document now returns `Err("unexpected character '}' at line 2 column 7 (char 15)")`, the same position CPython names. **Position rule corrected 2026-09-04 (TICKET-057).** This row's claim "the same position CPython names" held for the one document measured and was false for every MULTI-character token — in a 1086-document mutation sweep over 7 seeds, 242 of the 918 documents both implementations reject named where the scan STOPPED. Now 26, all of them CPython 3.14 trailing-comma diagnostics; two further families (invalid escape, leading zero) diverge deliberately and are recorded in TICKET-057. | Adjacent to **W8-22** (a caught `Error` carrying the line/col it came from) but strictly smaller: the parser already knew the offset it stopped at — this was threading it into the message, not a new span mechanism |
| ~~**W8-37**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-27 (TICKET-010) — `chezzi test` now routes all three diagnostics through the same renderers `run`/`check` use.** Measured, same three pairs, before → after: **fault** — `ERROR t (f37_test.chz:2) index 9 out of bounds (len 1)` with no frames → `ERROR t (f37_test.chz:2:12) index 9 out of bounds (len 1)` plus `  at boom (called at f37_test.chz:6:5)`; **type error in a test file** — `ERROR h37_test.chz` then the bare message with no line/col → `ERROR h37_test.chz` then `type error (h37_test.chz:2:14): cannot assign str to variable of type int`; **warning** — `warning (line 5, col 5): …` with no filename → `warning (w_test.chz:5:5): …`. Fix routes through `CheckError::render(path)` (checker diagnostics) and a new `vm::format_frames`/`Vm::take_fault_trace` (fault traces); `--errors=json` is unchanged (still `line` only, no `col`). Original report: **`chezzi test` is the least-instrumented surface in the toolchain — it drops the fault trace, the warning's filename, and the type error's line/col that `run`/`check` both print** | **This corrects W8-17's "did NOT reproduce" note**, which recorded *"`chezzi test` drops the line/col that `chezzi check` has"* as false: it is false on the in-test diagnostic path that was measured there, and **true on the file-error path** above — the report and the correction were each right about a different path. Weight: this is the project's **own primary loop** (639 Chezzi tests, and CLAUDE.md's testing policy pushes behavioural tests here), so it is the surface where a bad message cost the most. The W8-14/W8-15 span work already built everything needed — `test` was simply the sink that was not swept, the wave-6 meta-finding (*a fix applied to some arms of an N-way set*) one more time. Landed after **W8-13** (a failing `assert` printing its operands); same file, same loop |
| ~~**W8-38**~~ | `:236` | **`std.flag` accumulates positionals across `parse()` calls and has no `--help`.** `fs.parse(["a"])` → `Ok(['a'])`, then `fs.parse(["b"])` on the same `FlagSet` → **`Ok(['a', 'b'])`**; Go's `FlagSet.Parse` replaces (`[a]` then `[b]`, measured go1.26.6). Note the module header's own `ponytail:` comment scopes v1 deliberately (no subcommands, no required-flags) — neither of these two is on that deferral list, so they are defects rather than scope. Pairs with **W8-10** (`chezzi run` cannot forward args at all), which is the other half of "you cannot build a CLI with this yet" **CLOSED 2026-08-28 (TICKET-012).** `parse` now rebinds `self.pos = []` at the top of the call, and `--help`/`-h` return `Err("flag: help requested\n" + usage())` unless the caller registered their own `help`/`h` flag, which wins. |
| ~~**W8-39**~~ | `:236` | **FIXED 2026-08-27 (TICKET-011) — the `Err` names the directory that actually failed; `docs/reference/stdlib.md` corrected in the same change.** `fs.walk` blames the ROOT for an unreadable descendant, and the doc claims the opposite. With `wtest/sub` at mode 000, `fs.walk("wtest")` → `Err('wtest: Permission denied (os error 13)')` — naming `wtest`, which is readable. `docs/reference/stdlib.md:846` states *"`Err` on an unreadable **root**"*, so a reader takes the message at face value and checks the wrong directory. Go's `filepath.WalkDir` hands the *failing path* to the callback and lets the walk continue | A message-accuracy bug with a doc that vouches for the wrong reading — the **W8-18** shape (prose that over-claims costs more than prose that under-claims), except the code half is the one that is wrong here. Fix: name the path that actually failed. The larger decision (abort the whole walk vs. report-and-continue like Go) can be deferred, but the path in the message cannot — a wrong path in an error is a wrong answer |

| ~~**W8-42**~~ | `:236` | **CLOSED 2026-08-29 (TICKET-022).** `{x:spec}` now accepts all four remaining CPython-accepted forms: the `#` alternate form (`#x`/`#X`/`#o`/`#b`), `g`/`G` general format, `=` sign-aware fill, and a leading-space sign — plus the explicit `-` sign in the same slot, matching CPython 3.14.7 (`,`/`_` grouping landed earlier, 2026-08-29, TICKET-018). `{f:d}` (a `d` type char applied to a float) stays a deliberate **static** reject per this ticket's `## Decisions`, where CPython raises at runtime — a timing difference, not a semantic one.
