So: **(1)** `09622b25`→`fa99b2c3` made `satisfies(ty, "Eq")` mean what `==` accepts (containers, structs, enums, newtypes, tuples, `Option`/`Result`, `bytes`), pinned by the `intrinsic_grants_all_have_vm_arms` ratchet, which RUNS `x.eq(y)` on every newly-granted kind on both engines; **(2)** `9546706a` inserted the guard at `src/checker/pattern.rs:2485`, gating **both** operands (the `int`/`float` and `bytes`/`bytearray` cross arms recurse in, so a one-sided gate gives `Box(1) == Box(1.0)` and its mirror different verdicts) and decorating the existing message rather than replacing it; **(3)** `caaa5fcc`+`68f916fc` closed `W7-45`. **A green suite could not have caught any of this: five Criticals and four Importants were found across four adversarial-review rounds while the full suite stayed green throughout (4233/0 at the Stage-1 gate commit, 4236/0 at the end)** — every one by running the PRE-change binary on the `ok()` NEIGHBOURS the premise implies, then re-verifying the repro on the real binary on both engines. The Criticals were, in order: the new predicate FAILED OPEN and its `None` fed a GRANT (three faces — unsubstituted declared field types, a name-capture that re-introduced the very class this row closes, and a context-dependent verdict); a `structs.get(name)?` instead of the miss-only `struct_shape`, so a named-fn import skipped the field walk while `import … as L` on the identical program rejected; the re-entrancy cap declined in the granting direction; and TWO cycle guards that disagreed about what a type IS (one keyed on a bare name, one on `Ty::to_string()`), merged into one identity-keyed mechanism. A per-query memo turned a 2^N re-walk (N=26: 37.6s, vs 0.003s on prehead) back into linear. **The M23 rule "a type defining `compare` must define `eq` too" was DROPPED, not re-homed** — its premise was falsified by measurement in BOTH directions: a field-complete `compare` that agrees with structural `==` exactly was a FALSE REJECT, while a subset-`compare` plus an `eq` (this repo's own motivating example, `tests/chz/spec/eq_protocol_test.chz:1-11`) slipped through, so it never enforced coherence — it enforced "you typed the word `eq`". Rust permits manual `Ord` + derived `Eq`. Measured: a `struct Ver` with `compare` and no `eq` was two errors on prehead (*"type Ver does not satisfy Eq (missing method 'eq')"*) and is accepted now. **Ordering was NOT widened** — no default structural `compare`; Go has no struct `<`, Rust needs `#[derive(Ord)]`, Python `@dataclass` defaults `order=False`. **Residuals: `W7-52`** (`Ty::Protocol` — a fail-open hole in the `Eq` walk that cannot simply be refused, plus the existential-operand leak), **since RESOLVED 2026-08-12 as ancestor-correct** (the runtime-fault deferral — Go's own interface `==` does the same thing) **AND FIXED 2026-08-12** (a `[T: Eq]` bound over a protocol-typed value was still wrongly rejected — see the row), **`W7-53`, since FIXED 2026-08-12** (the erased call site — see the row), and **`W7-54`, since FIXED 2026-08-12** (a function value did not satisfy `Eq`, though `f == g` worked — `Ty::Func` now has an `intrinsic_recv_kind` and a granted `("Eq", "eq", "func")` row) |
| ~~**W7-40**~~ | `:8743` | **FIXED 2026-08-10 (NARROWED — a first attempt was measured to cause two hangs and was reverted). `std.net`'s blocking ops worked only on an M:N worker fiber, so the hello-world TCP server could not be written.** `l.accept()` from top-level `main` on the **default engine** returned `Err('accept would block: std.net sockets require the --parallel engine')` *while running the parallel engine*; same inside an eager `Executor` job. Cause: `Vm::mn` is assigned in **exactly one place** (`Vm::spawn_shell`, `src/vm/sched.rs:877`; `Vm::new` sets `mn: None`, `exec.rs:169`), so `mn.is_some()` means "this Vm is an M:N worker SHELL", **not** "the parallel engine is on" (that is `Vm::parallel`, default `true`) — and neither `main` nor an eager job has it. Go, measured: `ln.Accept()` on the **main goroutine** blocks and succeeds at 50.5 ms. **What shipped is one predicate**, `Vm::may_block_socket_in_place` (`src/vm/netio.rs:2044`) = `(mn.is_some() && native_reentry > 0)` (the pre-existing in-callback demote) `\|\|` `(parallel && eager_core.is_none() && is_counted_party())` — and `is_counted_party()` (`:1985`) is `owns_os_thread() && native_reentry == 0` with `owns_os_thread()` (`:2000`) = `mn.is_none() && mn_enlist_sched.is_none() && scheduler_stack.is_empty()`, so **the second disjunct is top-level `main` on the default engine and nothing else**. The four ops (`netio.rs:617`, `:817`, `:959`, `:1089`) restore the verbatim pre-existing `Err` for every other context. **Scope: `accept`/`read`/`read_bytes`/`write` from top-level `main` on the default engine now BLOCK and succeed; `--serial` and eager `Executor` jobs keep the immediate `Err`** | **The first attempt blocked everywhere and was measured as two rc=124 hangs.** **R1:** `--serial` has ONE thread, so blocking it starves the fiber that would make the fd ready — `timeout 15 chezzi run --serial examples/echo_server.chz` went rc=0 → **rc=124**, `socket_timeout.chz` printed `accept: timeout` then **rc=124**, with **no escape** (`--timeout` is rejected alongside `--serial`, `src/main.rs:660`). **R2:** an eager `Executor` job does NOT own its thread — it runs on the bounded process-wide pool (`pool::submit`, `src/vm/pool.rs:77`; sized by `vm::worker_count()`, `src/vm/mod.rs:304`, never grows) and the `mn.is_none()` path skipped `demote_socket_enter`, so no replacement worker spun: at `CHEZZI_THREADS=1` job A `accept`ing + job B `connect`ing → **rc=124** (≥2 threads passed), and it starves `parallel:` nurseries on the same pool. So **the `std.net` serial≠M:N divergence is NARROWED, NOT CLOSED** — R1 is the measured reason it must stay. Verified post-narrowing: `--serial` `echo_server`/`socket_timeout`/`echo_server_spawn` all rc=0, default engine unchanged, `CHEZZI_THREADS=1` executor shape rc=0 in 6 ms with a prompt `Err`, accept-on-`main` with an external `python3` peer prints `accepted`/`Ok('hi')` on the default engine and the `Err` on `--serial`; `cargo test --lib` 3952 passed, clippy clean; and the narrowed behavior is now **pinned by tests asserting the `Err` verdict on `--serial` and inside an `Executor` job**. **Lesson — a change that NARROWS when an error is returned is not tested by the suite that passed before it:** the full suite was green with BOTH regressions present, because nothing tested sockets on `--serial` and nothing tested a socket op inside an `Executor` job under a constrained pool — the suite has no example of the context such a change newly affects. Caught by adversarial review running the PRE-change binary; not by 4164 tests, not by two-engine parity, not by clippy. `W7-41`'s twin, same day and same branch |
| ~~**W7-39**~~ | `:8623` | **FIXED 2026-08-10 — a nested `Executor`'s jobs never observed the outer `shutdown_now()`.** An outer job creating an inner `Executor` and submitting an 8 s sleeper: pre-fix `done` then **`nap finished`** at **8.005 s**, post-fix `done` only at **0.056 s** (flat control 0.055 s both ways; `--serial` was already correct at 0.054 s, so this brings M:N *into* engine agreement). Cause: `prepare_eager_job` (`src/vm/sched.rs:4050`) installed `worker.cancel` and left `cancel_outer` empty — **the one seam in the tree that installed a cancel token without the `scope_ancestors()` half** that `spawn_shell` (`:875`) and `run_one_fiber` (`:1575-1585`) both pair it with — so an inner executor's jobs had chain `[inner.cancel]` and `drain_live_executors` joined the un-shut core at exit | Fixed at `sched.rs:4075-4082`, gated on `eager_core` so decision A2's "an `Executor` is **detached**" survives: detached from NURSERIES, not from an enclosing executor job (a `submit` from `main` or a `parallel:`/`spawn` fiber installs nothing), and `scope_ancestors()` already severs inside a `defer` so `defer ex.shutdown()` still submits uncancelled cleanup. **The ancestors are SPLIT and did not decide it** — CPython 3.14.6 nested `ThreadPoolExecutor` with `shutdown(cancel_futures=True, wait=False)` returns the CALL at 0.051 s but the PROCESS takes **8.037 s and prints both job lines** (an external report's "63 ms" measured the call, not the process); Go's `context.WithCancel` parent→child **propagates** at 50 ms. What decided it is Chezzi-vs-Chezzi: pre-fix the outer job's own identical `sleep_ms(8000)` WAS cancelled at 50 ms, so one spelling of one wait had two cancellation rules by nesting depth — `W7-16`'s ruling one level down. **Lesson: a one-line install is only safe once you have checked every path that WRITES the same field** — `run_one_fiber` overwrites both fields on every swap-in and misses this only because an eager job never enters the fiber scheduler **Follow-up, same branch:** the inherited chain is keyed on the executor's **creator** (`ExecutorCore::creator_cancel`, captured at `Op::NewExecutor`), never on the submitter, and it is **sticky** — never reset, matching Go's derived context. That made a `submit` after the creating job's cancellation vanish SILENTLY (`main` holding the only handle got `main done` rc=0 with the job never run, and its own graceful `shutdown()` waited for nothing); it now FAULTS through the existing post-shutdown reject — `submit on an Executor whose creating job was cancelled`. **Checkpoint trap:** a job with no cancellation point completes and hides the whole thing, so a repro without a `sleep_ms` is a false green. |
| ~~**W7-59**~~ | `:8757` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — `net.connect`'s in-flight arm stopped being a private sleep-spin, so the wait it does is the one every other blocking socket op does.** The `EINPROGRESS` arm gated on a bare `self.mn.is_some()` and everything else fell into `Vm::block_until_connected` — a 1 ms poll under a 10 s cap that bypassed `Vm::demote_block_socket` entirely, and with it every escape that loop owns: no cancel check, no `run_exit_err`, no `sched.terminate`, and on a worker shell no `demote_socket_enter` replacement worker. Measured on the shape that cost the most: an eager `Executor` job dialling the TEST-NET-1 black hole `192.0.2.1:9` with an outer `shutdown_now()` at 200 ms — **10 009 ms → 209 ms** | **Three arms, and the middle one is the whole point.** (1) an M:N fiber still parks on the netpoller, unchanged. (2) `self.eager_core.is_some()` — an eager `Executor` job, whose thread belongs to the bounded process-wide pool and has no scheduler under it to spin a replacement — returns the four sibling ops' verbatim `Err("connect would block: std.net sockets require the --parallel engine")`. (3) everywhere else blocks through `demote_block_socket` (`Interest::Write`, `attempt` = `finish_connect` + `peer_addr`), gaining `--timeout`, cancel, `os.exit` (`W7-47`) and terminate escapes, plus the replacement worker on a worker shell. `block_until_connected` is **deleted**. **The gate is deliberately NARROWER than the siblings' `Vm::may_block_socket_in_place`, and that predicate — the shape THIS ROW ITSELF proposed while it was open — is the wrong turn that was avoided:** it refuses `--serial` too, and on Linux a non-blocking `connect` reports `EINPROGRESS` even to a **LIVE loopback listener** (measured), so the old arm's "connected synchronously (the common loopback case)" comment was false and the wide gate would have broken `--serial` `net.connect` outright — pre-fix `--serial` connect+write WORKED and only `read` refused; the wide gate makes `connect` itself refuse. The asymmetry has a reason: `accept`/`read` wait on a **chezzi peer fiber** that can only run on the very thread they would block, so refusing on `--serial` prevents self-starvation (`W7-40` R1); a `connect` handshake is completed by the **KERNEL**, starves no chezzi party, and **both ancestors block** — measured CPython `socket.connect` **0.1 ms**, Go `net.Dial` **314 µs**, each from the sole/main thread. **Tests** (`src/vm/parity_tests.rs`): `connect_on_serial_main_still_reaches_a_live_listener` is the capability fence — verified RED under the wide gate (`CERR:connect would block: …` instead of `connected\nwrote`); `connect_inside_an_executor_job_errs_instead_of_pinning_a_pool_worker` verified RED pre-fix (`ERR:connect failed: timed out` after 10 s). `net_connect_top_level_dead_port_errors_not_hangs` was rewritten to assert `e.message()` on **both** engines — it previously discarded the message and so passed identically before and after, which is why it never detected any of this |
| ~~**W7-60**~~ | `:9709` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — an `Executor` join now observes `--timeout` and cancel, so a job parked in a nested join stops outliving the run that gave up on it.** **The row as filed was partly STALE and is corrected here:** `W7-58` had already replaced the untimed `eager_cv.wait` with a `DEMOTE_POLL_BACKOFF` `wait_timeout` poll asking `quiesced_only_joins`, so the deadlock verdict was covered. What was genuinely missing — and what the function's own doc admitted — is that the join observed **neither `--timeout` nor cancel**. Measured red→green, both halves, using `process.run` deliberately (it is documented as having no cancellation checkpoint until it returns, so a `Channel`-blocked job would have been ended by the pre-existing verdict and a `time.sleep_ms` job by its own `W7-16` checkpoint — neither would have failed pre-fix): **cancel** — an outer `shutdown_now()` at 200 ms over a job parked in an inner `shutdown()` whose job runs `process.run("sleep 6")`: **6 011 ms → 213 ms**; **`--timeout`** — `chezzi test --timeout=500` over a job running `process.run("sleep 9")`: pre-fix `TIMED-OUT` only after **9 010 ms**, with the "could not be aborted" suffix, post-fix **509 ms**. This closes a violation of Chezzi's OWN documented contract — `docs/reference/stdlib.md`'s "a scope cancel or an `Executor.shutdown_now()` ends the wait within ~5 ms" promise did not hold once the blocked party was a nested JOIN | **Fix:** `Vm::join_eager_jobs` (`src/vm/sched.rs`) runs the `Vm::block_halt_check` rungs in its poll loop, in the canonical order **`--timeout` > cancel > deadlock verdict**. Neither new rung is gated on `is_counted_party()` — they are facts about the **RUN**, not about who may judge it; only the verdict keeps that gate, exactly as `block_halt_check` gates its own three. Both are evaluated while the `eager` lock is **HELD**, deliberately: neither takes a lock, so there is no inversion to avoid, and holding it removes the window in which a cancel could be observed, latch `self.cancelled = true`, and then be discarded as stale — which would leave the fiber permanently `cancel_suppressed`, no-opping every later checkpoint. Every bail-out goes through one `Option<RuntimeError>` and takes **NO slots** (the jobs that own them are still outstanding and would `finish` into a vec this thread had emptied). **Three defects in the first cut, all found by adversarial review and all reproduced before acceptance — two of them charged independently by both prosecutors.** **(A1)** the bail ended the WAITER without stopping the WORK: every other `--timeout`/cancel observation happens INSIDE a job, where `run_outcome` trips the executor's cancel, but this one is on the JOINER and stored nothing — and `Vm::do_call`'s blocking-native offload gates on `cancel_requested()`, so a job part-way through a sequence of blocking calls **started the next one after the run had already reported TIMED-OUT** (measured: a fresh subprocess spawned ~1 s after a `--timeout=300` run finished; the executor is already `shut`, so `drain_live_executors` never revisits it and the bail is the last chance to ask). Fixed by storing `core.cancel` on every bail. **(A2)** the bail returned `Vec::new()` and so skipped `reduce_task_slots`, the ONLY writer of a FINISHED job's buffered output (`W7-5c`) — measured: a job that printed and completed at 50 ms lost its line entirely. It cannot `take_slots()` (outstanding jobs hold indices into that vec), so a new length-preserving `EagerState::take_finished` flushes the finished slots and leaves the rest; `reduce_task_slots` now tolerates the `None` those leave. **(A3)** the first cut's stated reason for omitting an `os.exit` rung was **false** — it claimed `W7-47` makes `outstanding()` drop, which holds only for a job that HAS a checkpoint; measured, `os.exit(3)` beside a `process.run("sleep 5")` job exits after **5.014 s**. The rung is still omitted, but for the real reason: it would unblock the waiter while the uninterruptible child kept running, which is `docs/reference/stdlib.md`'s documented blocking-native ceiling, not something a join can lift. **Accepted collateral:** a cancel bail leaves the joined executor marked `shut` with jobs outstanding, so the exit drain skips it. Not a new class — the pre-existing deadlock bail-out leaves the same state — and it is what `shutdown_now`'s documented "ask running jobs to stop **cooperatively**" means for a job that is itself parked in a join |
| ~~**bare `spawn:` start time**~~ | `:9245` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — a `spawn`ed task now starts at the `spawn`, like Go's `go f()`, instead of at its nursery's join.** Measured on the release binary: `print("A")` / `spawn: print("SPAWNED")` / `time.sleep_ms(300)` / `print("B")` went **`A B SPAWNED` → `A SPAWNED B`** on `chezzi run`, which is what the paired Go program (`go fmt.Println(...)`, go1.26.5) prints. `ch := Channel[int]()` / `spawn: ch.send(1)` / `print(ch.recv())` went from faulting **`recv on an empty channel: deadlock`** to printing **`1`**. The `spawn`-based TCP repro that surfaced the row — `l.accept()` on the accepting frame with the dialer in a bare `spawn` — hung forever pre-fix and now completes | **Shipped as `docs/design/roadmap.md` §2c1; read that for the mechanism, the FOUR measured wrong turns (three of them in the deadlock verdict — that, not starting the task early, is where the difficulty lived), and the corrections to what the plan predicted.** The join keeps its own orthogonal job: it guarantees **completion** by the barrier, never that a task could not have started (and printed) earlier. **Engine split, deliberate:** eager on M:N (the default `chezzi run`), **lazy on `--serial`** — one cooperative thread cannot run the task beside the body, so Go's semantics are unreachable there by construction, and that engine is slated for removal (§2b). This is the second place the two engines deliberately disagree; the first is `Executor.submit` (decision D3, `docs/reference/stdlib.md`). Collateral, documented: the `"{n} pending task(s) cancelled on early exit from parallel:"` report is **deleted** — with eager start there are no unstarted tasks to count and any residual number would be racy; `trio` and `asyncio.TaskGroup` print nothing here either. Tasks are still cancelled, silently |
| ~~**W7-38**~~ | `:8155` | **FIXED 2026-08-07 — the adversarial review (prosecute → defend → judge) of the `W7-30`–`W7-37` series found the series' OWN theme, "a real bug must never be reportable as a non-finding", still open in three places the series itself had just written or touched: the arg parser, the abort path, and the CI gate.** (1) `parse_range`'s guard rejected only INVERTED ranges, so `difffuzz --seeds 5..5` printed `done: 0 seeds 5..5, 0 finding(s) []` and exited **0** — a clean pass over zero comparisons, verbatim `W7-34`'s false negative one layer up (its message already claimed to reject "empty/inverted"). (2) `process::exit(2)` fired INSIDE the seed loop, so the abort printed no `done:` line and no histogram — losing the count of seeds that did run on the run where it matters most — and masked a real finding's exit **1** behind exit **2**. (3) `fuzz_range_cfg` printed its histogram with `eprintln!`, which libtest CAPTURES on a passing test, so a 120-seed sweep where all 120 timed out was green and indistinguishable from 120 real comparisons — the hole the histogram was added to close, unclosed exactly where it matters. Plus the mirror-image defect: `classify`'s `chz.code.is_none() => HostPanic` could not tell a SIGSEGV from a **SIGKILL**, so the cgroup OOM-killer under the `MemoryMax=6G` scope this repo's own CLAUDE.md mandates reported an OOM as a real Chezzi crash | Fixed as one commit: `end <= start` in BOTH bins; the abort now `break`s and always prints `done: {ran} of {total} …` + the histogram, with findings winning the exit code; both CI gates assert a hard **zero-comparisons floor** (deliberately not a "too many timeouts" threshold — an uncertain heuristic must decline, `W7-12`); `Capture` gained `signal: Option<i32>` in both oracles, only crash signals (SIGILL/SIGTRAP/SIGABRT/SIGBUS/SIGFPE/SIGSEGV/SIGSYS) stay `HostPanic`/`Crash` and every other signal declines into the fatal-but-not-a-finding `HarnessError`, with both reports NAMING the signal. Also: the hang tests' `locate_chezzi_for_test` no longer falls back to PATH (a 10-day-stale `~/.cargo/bin/chezzi` could have been what went green), and `gen_float`'s exactness proof was replaced by the real reason the float sweep is sound (IEEE-754 determinism). A seventh charge — a hang-verdict flake — was DISMISSED with evidence |
| ~~**W7-35**~~ | `:7845` | **FIXED 2026-08-07 — `panicfuzz` had the identical `W7-34` bug: `run_one`'s `.spawn().ok()?` and the staging `write_file` failure both collapsed "the child never even started" into the same non-finding `Outcome::Timeout`.** Ported `W7-34`'s exact shape: `run_one` now returns `Result<Capture, RunErr>`, a new `Outcome::HarnessError(String)` is fatal to both callers (`tests/panicfuzz.rs`'s new `fuzz_range_cfg` panics, `src/bin/panicfuzz.rs` exits **2** — distinct from **1** for real findings). See its session-log section for the other-holes audit (timeout-vs-crash was already right; the "nothing counts seeds actually executed" gap it shared with `difftest` was closed by the `W7-34` residual the same day — both binaries now print an outcome histogram). Whole-branch review then REVERSED this fix's one deliberate deviation: `classify` kept `Option<Capture>`, which leaves the very bug this row fixes expressible by a second caller (`classify(run_one(..).ok())`) — it now takes `Capture` like `difftest`'s, making it unrepresentable rather than merely fixed |
| ~~**M24-1**~~ | `Type-system / construction` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — the forwarding charge stopped being two name-only questions over the whole body and became one question per CALL SITE.** A generic with a static-carrying bound was charged a hidden witness parameter — and so lost the fn-VALUE position — for a call it never makes. **(a) Concrete-only forward.** `fn concrete[T: Default](x: T) -> int: return reset(Counter(1)).n` — nothing of its own `T` flows into `reset`. Measured: pre-fix `g := concrete` → `type error: 'concrete' cannot be used as a function value: its bound on T requires a static protocol method…`; post-fix the file RUNS, and `g := concrete[Counter]` / `apply(concrete, Counter(4))` both return `100`. **(b) A struct method that merely SHARES A NAME with an imported witness-taking fn.** `fn tagged[T: Default](x: T) -> int: h := Holder(4); return h.reset() + lib.plain(1)` beside `lib`'s witness-taking `reset` — the module half asked "is `lib` a free name?" and "is some `obj.member` spelled `reset`?" independently, and `Holder.reset` answered the second. Measured: pre-fix `g := tagged` → the same rejection; post-fix the file RUNS and prints `42`. **Isolation control** (measured pre-fix, and the proof the charge came from the module name, not the call): deleting only ` + lib.plain(1)` made the identical file run and print `40` — the two spellings now agree | **The rule now.** `crate::compiler::FreeNames` records a `CallSite` (module, callee name, argument shape) for every bare / module-qualified call, and `Checker::call_forwards_a_witness` asks two things per site: does the callee take witnesses (a bare name through `self.functions`; a qualified one through the module's `ModuleSig` under **that exact callee name** — the pair, not two halves), and could this fn's own `T` be what flows in? It could not when every argument is provably closed — a literal, or an ident-headed call whose head names a **non-generic struct** and whose own arguments are closed — **and** every witness the callee takes occurs in the callee's PARAMETER types. That last clause is load-bearing: `fn conv[T: Default, U](a: U) -> T` called as `conv(1)` looks concrete but takes its `T` from the expected type, i.e. the caller's own — measured, that program runs, so dropping the clause would have been an under-charge. Everything unrecognised still CHARGES (an empty argument list, a turbofish, a local, a generic ctor head, an unknown callee): an under-charge is the unsafe direction. **The position this gives back is the fn-VALUE one, and it gives it back ACROSS MODULES too**: a qualified call to another module's witness-taking fn with only concrete arguments (`wlib.renew(Seed(1))`) charges nothing either — measured on `8922fba9`, that fn's `g := …` was still `cannot be used as a function value`. The `spawn`/`defer` STATEMENT target is NOT newly opened (M24-5 had already made a charged fn a legal target by pushing its witness after the declared args, and those programs ran on `8922fba9`), but un-charging changes how they LOWER — the call site now pushes no witness against a proto compiled without one — so both statement positions carry running `test fn`s in `tests/chz/spec/static_witness_test.chz` beside the cross-module one. **Declaration order does not matter**, but not because the derivation is monotone: `fn_sig` seeds its answer mid-hoist against an incomplete struct table, so a concrete-argument forward looks like a real one, and the hoist fixpoint REMOVES that charge on its first pass once the table is complete (measured: `fn a[T: Default](x: T) -> int: return b(Later(1))` with `struct Later` declared LAST is accepted as a function value only because of that removal). What makes the loop settle — and what its `fn_decls.len()` cap does and does not buy — is argued at the loop in `src/checker/setup.rs`. One under-charge is held shut by something OUTSIDE this rule and is worth knowing before touching either: `ty_param_in_params` asks whether a witness OCCURS in a parameter type, never whether that parameter was SUPPLIED, so `fn conv[T: Default](a: int, b: T? = None) -> T` called as `conv(1)` satisfies it on paper — it charges only because desugar splices the default into the call site first (`conv(1, None)`, and `None` is not a closed argument), and because a NON-inline default on a type-param-mentioning slot is refused outright (`conv() expects 2 argument(s), got 1`). The whole `.chz`+`.md` corpus was swept with both binaries — zero verdict changes outside the new tests |
| ~~**M24-2**~~ | `Type-system / construction` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — a nested body captures the hidden `$w:T` only when it can REACH a witness, instead of always.** Every closure / nested `fn` / `defer:` / `spawn:` body inside a witness-taking fn used to carry one `str` per witness whether it read it or not. `compiler::nested_body_needs_witness` now asks the nested body's ALREADY-COMPUTED free-name walk (no second walk) and keeps the entry only when the body can reach a witness. Measured on the capture lists themselves (`src/vm/tests.rs`, `m24_2_*`): a witness-free closure / `defer:` / `spawn:` block inside a charged fn goes `["x", "$w:T"]` → `["x"]`, while a sibling closure calling `T.default()` in the same frame keeps `$w:T` | **The invariant, which is what makes the checker need no mirroring rule:** the compiler captures `$w:T` whenever the body can reach a witness for `T`, so `FnComp::witness_ref` stays a SUPERSET of `Checker::witness_scope` (still carried into nested bodies unconditionally — a second narrowing rule there would provably never change an outcome). `$w:` is unspellable, so a body reaches a witness only by CALLING something that consumes one, and every call the walk passes lands in exactly one of three channels — which is what makes the disjunction exhaustive over calls: a direct `T.static(…)` parses as a `Field` on `Ident("T")`, so `T` is a free NAME; a callee `record_call_site` cannot classify at all raises `opaque_calls` and always answers yes; a classified call is put to `call_may_take_witnesses`, which answers NO in exactly ONE case — a **BARE-IDENT** callee `f(…)` whose resolved key is absent from `WitnessTable::fns`, the one head shape this walk resolves the way the emitter does. **EVERY field-headed call `X.f(…)` answers yes, whatever `X` names** — an imported module, a TYPE, a receiver value, or a module name shadowed by an enclosing binding — because a Task-5 MEMBER forward (`h.build[T]()`) names `T` only as a type ARGUMENT and heads the call with a receiver, invisible to both name channels. **The member way is why the obvious two-disjunct predicate is WRONG**: `fn outer[T: Default](h: Holder)` with `f := fn(): h.build[T]()` type-checks and runs today, and a name-only rule drops its capture and turns it into a hard `internal: no type witness in scope to forward` at compile time. **Two successive cuts tried to CLASSIFY that head and each shipped green with a check-ok/run-fault, which is why the predicate no longer classifies it at all** (final form 2026-08-15). Round 1 read a `(Some(m), f)` record as a module qualification whenever `m` named an import — but the walk's `bound` set holds only names bound INSIDE the nested body, so an enclosing parameter or local named `lib` / `math` is recorded identically to a real module qualification; `fn outer[T](v: T, lib: Holder)` + `f := fn(): lib.build[T](v)` regressed from `14` to a runtime `no type witness in scope`, byte-identical on both engines. Round 2 added a shadow test over the enclosing frame's capture snapshot — a VALUE-binding table, which a TYPE never enters, so a `struct`/`enum`/newtype whose name equals an imported module fell straight through to the module branch and dropped the witness anyway (measured on a capitalized module file beside a same-named struct, on `import std.math` + `struct math`, and on `import std.time` + `enum time`: `0` → runtime `no type witness in scope to forward as 'T' into the call to 'mk'`, both engines). The head's meaning is a question about enclosing scopes AND the type namespace, neither of which a syntactic free-name walk carries, so the answer is now unconditional yes for every field-headed call — the class is retired by construction rather than by a third guess. Being too narrow is at least always LOUD (`witness_ref` returns `None`), never a silent wrong value. The walk flattens nested bodies into their enclosing one, so a `T.default()` two levels down keeps the outer capture alive (tested). **How little of the space this covers, honestly:** every field-headed call — `h.build(…)`, `lib.f(…)`, `a.b.c()`, `xs[i].m()`, `f()()`, `a?.m()` — still keeps EVERY witness — the win is confined to nested bodies doing only arithmetic, indexing and free-fn calls. Making it exact would mean reading `WitnessTable::calls` by each call's method-name-token span, a span-containment coordinate the span-keyed-table-aliasing history says not to build for a bench-neutral row. **The CHECKER had the same member blind spot one layer up, and it is now closed too** (2026-08-14): `Checker::witness_params_of` asked its forwarding charge over free names and module-qualified `CallSite`s only, so a fn whose ONLY use of `T` was a member witness call was never charged — and its own `h.build[T](x)` was then REJECTED (*"no hidden type witness for 'T' is reachable at this call site"*), with no other way to write the program. Measured, identical before this row and at its first fix, now running: `fn f[T: Default](h: Holder, x: T) -> T: return h.build[T](x)` → `0`, likewise `h.build(x)` (inferred), `h.mk[T]()` (return-type-only witness), a member forward inside a closure, an enum host, and a method declared BELOW its caller. The walk gained a MEMBER channel (`FreeNames::member_calls`, opt-in so the compiler's per-capture-site walks never build it) carrying each member call's TYPE ARGUMENTS and every identifier occurring in an argument expression, and the charge asks TWO questions, BOTH required: does THAT ONE CALL SITE carry something of THAT ONE declaration's — a type argument naming one of the fn's WITNESS CANDIDATES (`h.build[T](x)`), or an argument mentioning one of its parameters whose annotation names one of its type params (`h.build(x)` with `x: T`, `h.build(xs[0])` with `xs: List[T]` — idents are collected at any depth, the charge direction) — AND is the method NAME declared witness-taking somewhere in the module graph (`Checker::witness_member_names`)? **BOTH halves are required because each shipped a REGRESSION on its own, one after the other, and the final rule is their conjunction (2026-08-15).** Cut 1 keyed the charge on the METHOD NAME across the whole graph, with pinnability AND-folded: one unpinnable witnessed method poisoned its name for every member call in every static-bounded generic in the program, BUILTIN methods included, so `struct Bin: fn get[T: Default](self) -> T` anywhere made `fn uses_get[T: Default](x: T, m: Map[str, int]) -> T: y := m.get("a"); return x` unusable as a function value — measured `3` → *"'uses_get' cannot be used as a function value"*, and likewise `xs.push(1)` on a `List[int]` against a `Sink.push[T: Default]` (`5` → refused). Cut 2 dropped the name index and asked the call site alone — which never looks at the RECEIVER, so a builtin method charged as readily as a witnessed one: `fn label[T: Default](x: T, sink: List[T]) -> int: sink.push(x); return sink.len()` (a `List.push`, which can never take a witness) went from RUNNING pre-branch to *"'label' cannot be used as a function value"*, and the charge then propagated to every caller forwarding its own `T` into `label`. ANDed they are strictly narrower than either: `m.get("a")` fails the call-site half (a literal argument names nothing of the caller's), `sink.push(x)` fails the name half unless a user type really declares a witness-taking `push` — measured, the identical body charges again the moment `struct Sink: fn push[U: Default]` is added to the file, which is the safe direction. A concrete member call still costs nothing (`p.build(1)` — measured `11` throughout), and neither does a builtin call, which is the point: the position an over-charge costs is the difference between compiling and not. **Still refused, deliberately:** (i) a `T` occurring in NEITHER a parameter type nor the return type of its own fn (`fn f[T: Default](h: Holder, k: int) -> int: q := h.mk[T]()`) — that is `ty_param_in_sig`, a PRE-EXISTING fence that answers identically on the free-fn channel (`q := empty[T]()` — measured rejected before and after), and lifting it needs the receiver/argument TYPES the hoist does not have; (ii) a member forward whose `T` reaches the argument through a LOCAL rather than a parameter (`v := x` then `h.build(v)`) — the name-keyed cut charged it, the per-call-site rule cannot see it, and it is the ONE shape that cut covered and this one does not (measured: rejected pre-branch, `0` under the name-keyed cut, rejected now). It is a loud rejection with a working spelling one turbofish away (`h.build[T](v)`), not a silent wrong value. Bench-neutral as filed — cost is one fewer capture entry per nested body that reaches no witness, not a measurable speedup |
| ~~**M24-3**~~ | `Type-system / construction` | **FIXED 2026-08-10 — a type parameter now SHADOWS a same-named type in static-call position, in both halves.** `fn f[Item: Tagged](x: Item) -> int: return Item.tag()` beside a `struct Item` used to answer `7` (the struct); Rust answers `1` (`fn f<Item: D>(_x: Item) -> i32 { Item::tag() }` with `f(Counter)`, measured 2026-08-10) and so does Chezzi now. The checker's type-param arm moved ahead of its enum/struct static arms and the compiler's witness arm ahead of its own bare-receiver arms — both halves agree, on the ancestor's answer this time. The earlier fix had aligned them on the struct, which is what "correctness outranks agreement" is about. A shadowing param whose bound does NOT declare the method now says so (rustc: *no associated function `tag` found for type parameter*) instead of silently reaching the struct. Pinned by `a_type_param_shadows_a_type_name_of_the_same_name` (`tests/chz/spec/static_witness_test.chz`, RUNS) + `witness_type_param_shadows_a_same_named_type_in_static_call_position` | **Residual CLOSED 2026-08-10 (round 4).** The one-spelling fix left the same name meaning two things in one expression (`Item.tag() * 100 + Item[int].tag()` → `107`: the param answered `1`, the struct `7`). The shadow is now a RULE with one derivation — `Checker::shadowing_type_param` — asked by every type-name position: the static call (lowers), and the type-level turbofish, the bare constructor and `Type.Variant` (all reject through `Checker::type_param_shadow_error`, since a type parameter is erased). rustc rejects the turbofish and the variant on the parameter too, measured 2026-08-10 (E0109 / E0599), and Go — the ONE-NAMESPACE ancestor, which is the relevant one for scoping — rejects the constructor and a same-named function call as well (`Item{}` → *invalid composite literal type Item*; `foo()` under `[foo any]` → *missing argument in conversion to foo*). Rust keeps `Item(99)` as the tuple-struct constructor only because its type and value namespaces are separate, which Chezzi's are not. The two dead-end diagnostics were also wrong and are fixed: an UNBOUNDED param no longer invents `fn tag(...) -> Self` for a `-> int` requirement (it names a protocol that already declares the static method, as rustc does), and an ENCLOSING-TYPE param is now recognised BEFORE the bound lookup, so it is never told to add a bound that cannot help. Pinned by `witness_type_param_shadows_in_every_type_name_position` + `witness_static_call_dead_ends_name_the_type_parameter`, and `the_shadow_rule_leaves_every_unshadowed_position_alone` (RUNS) |
| ~~**M24-4**~~ | `Type-system / construction` | **FIXED 2026-08-10 — the manifest-entrypoint witness rejection reaches every consumer that checks the file.** It used to be CLI state threaded only through bare `chezzi run`, so `chezzi check src/main.chz` printed *ok: no type errors* about a project that cannot start and the editor/LSP showed nothing. `manifest::entry_fn_for(file)` is now the ONE derivation — `Some(fn)` exactly when the project's `entrypoint = "<path>:fn"` resolves to that file — and `main::type_check_inner` + `editor::diagnostics` both read it. The entrypoint's shape is a property of the PROJECT, not of one invocation (Go rejects a `func main` with parameters at build time), so every consumer that STATICALLY CHECKS the file reports it. **Corrected 2026-08-10 (round 4):** it also fired on `chezzi run <file>`, which is script mode — it runs the top level and invokes nothing — so a project whose `main` takes a witness could no longer run its own entry file at all. The three CLI shapes are now spelled out as `main::EntryGate` (`Named` / `FromManifest` / `Script`) so a caller cannot mean two things by `None`. The gate also covers DECLARED parameters now: `fn main(a: int)` as the entrypoint used to check green and die at startup with *"function 'main' expects 1 argument(s), got 0"*. Pinned by `a_witness_taking_manifest_entrypoint_is_refused_by_check_too`, `a_manifest_entrypoint_taking_arguments_is_refused_at_check_time` and the strengthened `witness_taking_manifest_entrypoint_is_refused_with_its_reason` (`tests/module_root.rs`, all driving the real binary) | **Residual:** `editor::hover` / `checker::hover_type` take no entry fn, so a hover still answers on a program whose only error is this gate. Hover is advisory and its own "no type when the program doesn't check" contract is unaffected by which errors exist |
| ~~**M24-5**~~ | `Type-system / construction` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — a witness-taking generic is an ordinary `spawn`/`defer` STATEMENT target.** `defer reset(c)` used to be refused with *"'reset' takes a static-protocol bound (T), so it cannot be the target of `defer` yet — call it eagerly and `defer` the result, or wrap the call in a closure"* (same message with `spawn`); it now runs, and `chezzi run` prints `main body done` then `reset ran` — the deferred call after the body. It was arity plumbing, never erasure: the target is never read as a value. The six emit lines in `compile_spawn`/`compile_defer` now push the hidden trailing witness after the declared (or keyword-permuted) args and widen `Op::SpawnCall`/`SpawnMethod`/`DeferCall`/`DeferMethod`'s `argc` by that count, exactly as `Op::Call`/`CallMethod` already did; the checker's `reject_witness_spawn_defer_target` + `witness_indirect_target` refusal and the compiler's `reject_witness_indirect_call` backstop are gone. **The VM needed nothing** — `do_defer`/`do_spawn` split `argc` operands off the stack generically and a witness is a plain `str`, so it deep-copies across the spawn airlock like any other sendable value. Ancestor (Go 1.26.5, measured 2026-08-14): `func reset[T Defaulter[T]](old T, tag string) T` used as both `defer reset(c, "defer")` and `go reset(c, "go")` prints `go reset ran -> {7}` / `main body done` / `defer reset ran -> {7}` — Chezzi's ordering matches. Cover: `checker::tests::witness_fn_is_a_spawn_or_defer_target_ok` (+ the `_member_`/`_cross_module_`/`_forwarding_` siblings), `vm::tests::cross_module_witness_defer_target_runs_both_engines`, and 7 `test fn`s in `tests/chz/spec/static_witness_test.chz` (free fn, member, keyword spelling, forwarded witness, both engines). **Its sibling defect, found the same day, is fixed in the same place (was filed as M24-5b, now folded in):** a RECEIVER-LESS member head. `defer Holder.build(3)` / `spawn Gen[int].build(3)` PANICKED the compiler — `chezzi check` said *ok: no type errors*, `chezzi run` died with `compiler: global 'Holder' has no slot (checker should reject undefined names)` — because `Op::SpawnMethod`/`DeferMethod` record a RECEIVER value plus a member name and a static method has none, so the compiler compiled the TYPE NAME as a value. A `from`-imported head (`import Holder from lib`) slipped past that even as a diagnostic, since its global slot EXISTS holding `Nil`, and faulted late with `type nil has no method 'build'`; the module-qualified `lib.Holder.build(3)` faulted with `module 'lib' has no member 'Holder'`. **A static method is an ordinary function call, so it now WORKS** — the inconsistency was internal (`print(H.build(3))` and `defer print(H.build(3))` both worked while `defer H.build(3)` did not), and Go accepts the analogue (`defer pkg.F(x)`, `defer Holder.Make(h, 2)`, measured go1.26.5). One predicate, `Compiler::receiverless_call_head`, routes every static spelling (bare, both turbofish carriers, combined `Type[T].m[U]`, `from`-imported, module-qualified, and the `$w:T` witness head) through a synthetic wrapper proto that replays the call via `compile_call` — so each reaches exactly the bytecode its eager form emits, with eager args and LIFO `defer` preserved and no new opcode or VM change. A CONSTRUCTOR head (`defer E.A(3)`, `defer lib.Pt(3)`) is instead rejected by the **CHECKER**, with the message and the phase the bare `defer P(3)` rule already used — the interim compiler refusal was itself the checker-superset-of-compiler class, green in `chezzi check` and in the editor. Cover: `checker::tests::a_dotted_constructor_is_not_a_spawn_or_defer_target_rejected` + `a_dotted_static_method_is_a_spawn_or_defer_target_ok`, `vm::tests::a_static_method_head_is_a_spawn_or_defer_target_runs_both_engines` + `module_reached_static_heads_are_defer_targets_both_engines`, and 7 more `test fn`s in `tests/chz/spec/static_witness_test.chz` (defer + spawn, LIFO, eager-arg snapshot, turbofish head, witness head). **The constructor rule's LAST missing spelling closed 2026-08-14, same place, same commit family:** a native constructor reached through its std MODULE object — `defer concurrency.Shared(0)` (likewise `RwShared`/`Atomic`/`AtomicInt`/`Executor`, whole-module or aliased) and `defer time.timer(10)` — was `chezzi check`-CLEAN and then faulted at run time with `runtime error (line 4, col 11): module 'std.concurrency' has no member 'Shared'` (measured on the pre-branch binary too — pre-existing, not a regression), while the bare `defer Shared(0)` spelling was refused correctly by the checker. One concept, two verdicts, and the qualified one was a check-clean program that cannot run: the checker-superset-of-compiler class again. `Checker::dotted_ctor_target` now carries a fourth arm mirroring `infer_call`'s qualified-native-ctor arm, the two sharing one `Checker::qualified_native_ctor` name set so they cannot drift; the type-only native names (`net.Socket`, the FFI widths, `ptr`) are deliberately excluded because `infer_call` already gives them a better, check-time message (*"has no constructor — it is a type-only native type"*). An ordinary module FUNCTION is untouched — `defer math.abs(-3)` still checks and still runs on both engines. Cover: `checker::tests::a_qualified_native_constructor_is_not_a_spawn_or_defer_target_rejected` (7 ctor spellings × defer/spawn) + `a_native_module_function_is_a_defer_target_ok`, and `tests/chz/stdlib/math_test.chz::deferred_native_module_fn_runs`. **The LAST asymmetry in the family closed 2026-08-14, same commit family:** a module-qualified function was a legal `defer` target but NOT a legal `spawn` target — `spawn lib3.helper(3)` was refused at check time with *"type error (line 5, col 15): cannot spawn on a non-sendable receiver of type module lib3"* (likewise `spawn math.abs(-3)` → `module math`, an aliased `import std.math as m` → `module m`, a witness-taking `spawn lib.reset(c)`, and the bare no-nursery spelling), while `defer lib3.helper(3)` / `defer math.abs(-3)` both checked clean and ran. A module is a NAMESPACE, not a receiver value: the spawn path ran the shape through the *"spawn on a receiver"* sendability sweep and asked whether the MODULE could cross the airlock — a question about the wrong thing. Ancestor (Go 1.26.5, measured 2026-08-14): `go fmt.Fprintln(os.Stdout, …)` and `defer fmt.Fprintln(os.Stdout, …)` as bare statement targets both print; a package-qualified `go pkgTarget(&wg)` runs. Fixed in the two places the shape is decided, NOT by special-casing the message: the checker's receiver sweep (`sig.rs`) skips exactly `Ty::Module`, and `Compiler::receiverless_call_head` now answers yes for a module head too, so the call is replayed through the wrapper proto its eager spelling emits instead of pushing the module HANDLE as a `SpawnMethod` receiver. **Both legs were needed** — the checker-only cut traded the check error for a *"this task value can't cross a worker boundary … a module handle cannot cross"* runtime fault (the checker-superset-of-compiler class). **Real sendability is NOT weakened:** the skip is keyed on an UNBOUND MODULE NAME — the same two clauses `receiverless_call_head` lowers on, so check-time and lowering cannot disagree — so a container merely holding a module (`xs := [math]` → `spawn xs.clear()` → *"cannot spawn on a non-sendable receiver of type List[module math]"*), a local that SHADOWS a module name (`math := [math]`, same message), a local BOUND to the module (`m := math` → `spawn m.abs(-3)` → *"cannot spawn on a non-sendable receiver of type module math"*), and every non-sendable ARGUMENT (`spawn take(math)` → *"cannot pass a non-sendable value of type module math to a spawned task"*) are refused with byte-identical messages before and after. **Keying it on the RESOLVED `Ty::Module` instead was itself a regression, fixed 2026-08-15:** a module bound to a local is `Ty::Module` but not a namespace head, so `chezzi check` said *ok: no type errors* and `chezzi run` faulted with *"a module handle cannot cross"* — the checker-superset-of-compiler class, one commit after the fix that introduced it (`checker::tests::spawn_on_non_sendable_receiver_still_rejected` now pins both bound spellings). Cover: `checker::tests::spawn_on_module_qualified_fn_ok` + `spawn_on_non_sendable_receiver_still_rejected` + `spawn_non_sendable_argument_still_rejected` + the flipped `witness_fn_is_a_cross_module_defer_target_ok`, and 4 `test fn`s in `tests/chz/spec/spawn_module_target_test.chz` (user module, aliased import, bare `spawn` with no nursery, native module — each asserting the spawned call's OBSERVABLE effect, both engines) | — |
| ~~**M24-6**~~ | `Diagnostics / spans` | **FIXED 2026-08-11 — an interpolation fragment's reported position is now the fragment's REAL physical source position, line and column, at any nesting depth.** Mechanism: the lexer builds a per-literal sparse checkpoint map (`PosMap`, content-char index → source `Span`) and hangs it on the `str` token (`Token::Str(StrLit { raw, map })`); a fragment is re-lexed by `lexer::tokenize_frag(src, map, off)`, whose `span_at` asks the map instead of counting columns. **One rule builds it: `note(idx, at)` records a position only when it is NOT what the previous checkpoint already implies** — called per content char at the top of `string()`/`triple_string()`, with no case analysis over escape kinds. Per-char is load-bearing, not tidiness: a literal nested inside a fragment builds its own map out of `span_at`, i.e. out of the PARENT's map, so a parent `\t`/`\u{…}` escape's column jump is invisible to the inner lexer and any "record at escapes and newlines" shortcut would put the inner fragment one column left of the truth. Measured, `chezzi run`/`check`, pre-change binary vs post-change: `s := """aaa\nbbb\ncc {10 / z} dd"""` (real newlines) **(2,19) → (4,5)**; the same literal with `\n` ESCAPES **(2,19) → (2,21)**; `print("{ f('a\tb{nope}c') }")` on line 3 **(3,17) → (3,18)**. The first two are the punchline: they were the SAME wrong answer, because `raw` is the post-escape payload and the old design could not tell a real newline from a `\n` escape. The `"""`-short-by-2 and per-escape drifts disappear for free — checkpoint 0 is taken on entry to `string()`/`triple_string()`, where `self.pos` already sits past the opening delimiter. Cost, stated exactly (adversarial review caught the first cut claiming more than it delivered — `PosMap::flat` used to `vec![]` unconditionally, so a brace-free literal of 2M `\t` escapes built and dropped **43 MB**, measured by peak RSS): checkpoint 0 is held INLINE on the map, so a literal with no escapes and no newlines leaves `pts` empty and an empty `Vec` does not heap-allocate — the common case is genuinely free. A literal WITH escapes pushes one checkpoint per discontinuity, and `map` is kept only if the literal contains `{` or `}` (the same guard desugar uses), so a brace-free escape-heavy literal still builds a small `Vec` and drops it — work proportional to escapes, not to length, and avoidable only with a second escape-aware pre-scan (`ponytail:` noted at the call site, not worth it until a profile says so). `raw`/`RawStr`/`Bytes` scanners get no map at all. `StrLit`'s `Debug` is hand-written to print as the bare `String`, so `chezzi tokens` / `chezzi ast` output is byte-identical — a derived `Debug` had turned one interpolated literal into a screenful of `Span` blocks (also adversarial review). Runtime-inert — the `.chz` suite is green on both engines. **Retractions of this row's own claims.** (a) "Exact positions are not recoverable here" — they are; `raw` is lossy but the LEXER is not, and recording as it scans costs a `Vec` of a handful of checkpoints. (b) The cost estimate "changes `Token::Str`, the AST node and every match site" — actual: **2 real parser edits** (`expect_str`, the pattern-literal arm, both splitting a `Token::Str | Token::RawStr` or-pattern), **3 signature-only** (`check_interpolation`, `compile_str`, `interp_exprs`), **~12 zero-edit sites** (`Deref<Target = str>` covers `raw.contains('{')` and friends), and ~22 mechanical `"…".to_string()` → `"…".into()` test literals. (c) The predicted true position "line 4, col 8" is wrong for this codebase: a binary expression's span anchors on its LEFT operand, so `10 / z` reports the `1` — **col 5**, verified against the equivalent plain (non-interpolated) program | **The invariant got STRONGER, which is why this was worth doing at all.** `PosMap::at` is injective on a literal's content range: checkpoints are `span_at` at strictly increasing `self.pos` (so distinct, increasing physical positions), and between two checkpoints `note` fired and DECLINED, which is exactly the statement that the run is a contiguous strictly-increasing column range on one line. So a fragment token's span is the true position of a real source char, and `WitnessTable`/`KeywordTable`/`CarrierTable` keys are injective by a property of the FILE rather than of an offset arithmetic we chose. The four hazards check out: two fragments of one literal occupy disjoint `raw` sub-ranges; a nested literal sits physically inside its parent's extent at a strictly later position (the `static_witness_test.chz` sibling collision becomes unrepresentable); there are no zero-width escapes (`\` + newline is a `LexError`); `{{`/`}}` are consumed as content, so a fragment's `raw` index stays true. That retires `2a27697e`'s trade — it bought non-aliasing with a monotone OFFSET that could run off the end of its physical line; this buys it outright. **Cross-ref `W7-49`**, which made the same move for module identity (a `Span` grew a `file` id instead of hoping spans would not repeat across modules): both replace "an encoding we believe is injective" with "the thing itself". Residual, filed and since **FIXED** (2026-08-14, next row): **M24-7** (`LexError` carried no column) **Class cross-ref (2026-08-16): a FOURTH instance of the span-keyed side-table aliasing class landed and was fixed — see the `span-keyed side table, instance #4` row. The class now reads M24-6 / W7-49 / W7-43 / ListWidenTable, and every fix made the coordinate REAL rather than re-anchoring a span.** |
| ~~**M24-7**~~ | `Diagnostics / spans` | **FIXED 2026-08-14 — a lex error points at the offending CHARACTER, on both axes, like every other diagnostic in the compiler.** Measured `chezzi check`, before → after: `z := "ab\u{12zz}cd"` on line 3 — *resolve error (line 3, col 1): lex error (line 3): invalid hex digit in unicode escape* → *resolve error (line 3, col 14): lex error (line 3, col 14): …* (the first `z` of `12zz`); `y := 0x` on line 2 — *(line 2, col 1) / lex error (line 2)* → *(line 2, col 6)* (the `0`); `y := "a\qb"` on line 2 — *(line 2, col 1)* → *(line 2, col 9)* (the `q`); `y := "abc` on line 2 of a 2-line file — *resolve error (line 3, col 1): lex error (line 3): unterminated string literal* → *(line 2, col 6)* (the opening quote), which also fixes the **LINE**: the cursor had run to EOF, so the position was read off the line the FILE ends on. **The position rule** (measured against the ancestors 2026-08-14, rustc 1.x + CPython 3.14; see the `lex_error_points_at_the_offending_character` table): a bad char inside a `\u{…}` escape → **the offending char** (rustc `badu.rs:2:20`); an unknown/invalid escape → **the escape char**, after the `\` (rustc `num.rs:3:16`); a malformed number → **the start of the numeric literal** (rustc `num.rs:2:13`), **except a misplaced `'_'` group separator, which points at the offending `_`** — CPython 3.14.6 measured: `y = 1__0` → *lineno 2 offset 6*, its first `_`, and `y = 0b1__0` → offset 8, likewise; **rustc has no opinion on this one, it ACCEPTS `1__0` silently** (measured 1.97.0, compiles clean — an earlier note here citing a rustc caret on the doubled underscore was wrong); an unterminated string/triple-string/byte-string → **the OPENING delimiter** (rustc `unterm.rs:2:13`, CPython puts its caret under the opening quote); a `\`+newline **line continuation** → **the `\`** (Chezzi's own choice — both ancestors *support* line continuations, so neither has a diagnostic to copy); an error about a `\u{…}` escape **as a whole** → **the escape's opening `{`** (see the divergence note below); everything else → the character the message is about. One line: *point at the offending character; an unterminated delimiter points at its opener.* **Where the whole-escape sub-family diverges from rustc, by 1-2 columns and deliberately:** measured rustc 1.97.0, `"x\uZ"` (incorrect escape), `"y\u{}"` (empty), a 7-hex-digit body (overlong) and `"w\u{D800}"` (surrogate) *all* anchor at the `\` and caret-**span** the whole escape; `LexError` carries a point, not a span, so Chezzi names a char *inside* the escape instead — the offending char where there is one (the `Z`, the 7th digit) and otherwise the `{`. Every one is a real character on the right line inside the construct the message is about, which is the bar ("never a confidently wrong column"); matching rustc's anchor exactly needs a span, not a column, so the code was left alone. | Mechanism: `LexError` gained `pub col: usize`, its `Display` is now `lex error (line {}, col {}): {}` (matching `impl Display for Span`), and the seam at `src/resolver/mod.rs:~691` reads `e.col` instead of the hardcoded `col: 1` — so the LSP squiggle, `--errors=json` and the plain-text rendering all land on the bad char with no edit of their own. `Lexer::error` stays the only constructor path, now a three-step funnel: `error()` → `error_at(pos)` → `error_span(span)`, with `error_at` asking `span_at` (which composes through the M24-6 per-literal `PosMap`, so a column inside a re-lexed interpolation fragment is right for free). All 35 construction sites were audited and moved to the position their family calls for. `error_span` exists because `span_at` derives the column as `pos - self.line_start` — it CANNOT recover a position on an earlier line, and an unterminated literal's opener usually is one; the six literal scanners capture their opener's `Span` at entry instead. `Span` itself is untouched (still 12 bytes). **Follow-up fix, same phase:** the string-**interpolation** seam was still throwing the new column away — `interpolation::parse_expr_str` built its `InterpError` from the *string literal's* span, so `s := "a\tb{1 + @}c"` on line 2 rendered *type error (line 2, col 6): lex error (line 2, col 16): unexpected character '@'*: the inner text named the `@`, the OUTER position (the one the LSP squiggles and `--errors=json` emits) named the opening quote. The `InterpError` now carries the `LexError`'s own line/col with the literal's `file`, `Checker::check_interpolation` reports `e.span` instead of the literal's (the compiler seam already did), and the message drops the doubled position — now *type error (line 2, col 16): lex error: unexpected character '@'*, `{"line":2,"col":16,…}` in JSON. Pinned by `a_lex_error_inside_a_fragment_reports_its_real_line`, which now asserts the OUTER `e.span`. **The PRIMARY path kept stuttering for one more day and is now consistent with it (2026-08-15):** `resolver::parse` still built its message from `LexError::to_string()`, so a module-graph lex error rendered *resolve error (line 1, col 8): in module 'dtest.badmod': lex error (line 1, col 8): unexpected character '@'* — the same position twice, once from the outer renderer and once from the inner `Display`. It now uses `e.message` under the same `lex error: ` prefix the interpolation seam uses. One seam, one rendering rule (`tests/check_errors_json.rs::lex_error_carries_a_real_column` asserts the whole line). **Second follow-up, 2026-08-14 — the seam's remaining four discards, closed:** the lex arm was one of *five* error paths out of `interpolation::parse_interpolation` and the other four all still reported the literal's opening quote. Each now names a real character, each measured against CPython 3.14. (1) A fragment **parse** error — `s := "a\tb{1 + }c"` → *(line 2, col 6)* → **(line 2, col 15)**, one past the last token the fragment parser consumed, which is the identical rule the TOP-LEVEL parser applies to the same message (`y := 1 + ` → col 10). `tokenize_frag` had already given every fragment token — EOF included — its real physical span, so `ParseError.span` was correct all along and the fix is literally `span: e.span`; CPython points inside the fragment too (`compile('y = f"a\tb{1 + }c"', …)` → *offset 14*, the `+`). No phase prefix was added to the message: unlike `LexError`, `ParseError.message` never carried a rendered position, so there is nothing to strip and nothing to stutter. (2) **Unterminated `{`** — `s := "a\tb{1 + c"` → col 6 → **col 17**, the literal's closing delimiter (`map.at(raw.chars().count())`); CPython measured *offset 17*, the same char. That index used to be EXTRAPOLATED from the last checkpoint, which is only the delimiter's position while the literal's final content char shares its line — **fixed 2026-08-15**, because when the last content char is a real NEWLINE the extrapolation runs off the end of the PREVIOUS line and reports a column the file does not have: `x := """a` / `{b` / `"""` said *(line 2, col 4)* of a two-character line, and four blank lines before the terminator drifted the caret to *(line 5, col 2)* of an empty one (fuzzing: 37/5000 interpolation inputs produced an out-of-range column, ALL from this one arm; every other new position site in the range stayed in range). The lexer now CHECKPOINTS one-past-the-end with the closing delimiter's own span (`PosMap::note`, called once more after the scan loop in both `string()` and `triple_string()`), so `at(len)` is a real position by construction — the two cases above now report *(line 3, col 1)* and *(line 6, col 1)*, the delimiter itself, and single-line literals are unchanged. Pinned by `checker::tests::unterminated_fragment_points_at_the_real_closing_delimiter`, which also asserts the reported column exists on the reported line. (3) **Unmatched `}`** — `s := "a\tb}c"` → col 6 → **col 11**, the `}` itself (`map.at(i)`); CPython measured *offset 11*, the same char. (4) **Format-spec error** — `s := "a\tb{x:>99999999}c"` → col 6 → **col 14**, the spec's first char; `fmtspec::parse` returns a bare `String` with no offset, and a bad format spec is a *runtime* `ValueError` in CPython (`f"{x:>99999999}"` compiles clean, measured), so there is no ancestor caret to copy — the rule taken is "inside the fragment, as precise as the available information allows", which the fragment's opening quote never was. **Audited and already correct, so untouched:** checker errors on a fragment *expression* (`"a\tb{nope}c"` → col 12, `"a\tb{1 + \"z\"}c"` → col 12) ride `tokenize_frag`'s spans through the AST; the compiler twin (`compile_str`) already mapped `span: e.span` and so inherited the fix for free; `desugar`'s two `parse_interpolation` callers and `compiler::interp_exprs` discard the error entirely. That closes the seam — every position out of `parse_interpolation` is now a real char inside the construct its message is about. Pinned by `checker::tests::a_parse_error_inside_a_fragment_reports_the_fragments_own_position` and `literal_shape_errors_point_at_their_own_char`, both carrying a real `\t` escape before the offending char so a column counted off the post-escape content rather than composed through the `PosMap` lands one to the left and fails. |
| ~~**W7-50**~~ | `Parser / LSP` | **FIXED 2026-08-13 — the two parser depth constants are re-derived from a measurement, the LSP's last unwrapped front-end entry point is on the big stack, and the margin is now an assertion instead of a prose claim.** As filed: `MAX_DEPTH = 64` had **one level** of margin (65 passes, 66 aborts on a ~2 MiB thread) and `editor::semantic_tokens` reached `parser::parse` on the LSP's own ~2 MiB tokio worker. **Half (b), the cheap one, landed first**: `semantic_tokens` hops onto `crate::on_frontend_stack` like `diagnostics`/`hover`, and `cmd_ast`'s `{:#?}` walk with it (`src/editor/mod.rs`, `src/main.rs:~133`) — so no production parse runs on a small stack any more, which is what let `MAX_DEPTH` rise at all. **Half (a) is a re-derivation, not a bump.** An `--ignored` probe (`checker::tests::stack_probe_frontend_walker_depth`) measures the two resources separately, debug build: parse recursion costs **7 753 B per `MAX_DEPTH` unit**, and the binding post-parse walker is `compile` at **12 162 B per AST node** (`desugar` 11 323, `check` 6 850). Against the SMALLER of the two big stacks — `vm::VM_STACK_BYTES` = 384 MiB, which `chezzi run` re-does `build_graph`+`compile_graph` on — that is ~51 900 depth units and ~33 100 AST nodes of budget. **One constant was being asked to bound two resources**, which is why no single value could reach CPython parity. Split: **`MAX_DEPTH` = 64 → 512** (parse recursion only; ~4 MB of 384 MiB, a **~100× margin**), and **`MAX_CHAIN_DEPTH` (500, per-loop) → `MAX_AST_DEPTH` = 16 000** (AST depth). **Measured before/after, release binary, vs CPython 3.14's own limit of 200:** parens **30 → 254**, nested lists **31 → 255**, nested calls **30 → 254**, nested `if` blocks **61 → 509** (CPython 99), unary **61 → 509**, generic types **62 → 510**, match patterns **60 → 508**; the multiplicative `f(g(…).f×498)` fixture **15 → 31**; the double-fold `( … .f×499 +1×499 )` shape **30 → 16**, the one form that NARROWS (see correction 2). **Three corrections the work turned up.** (1) **Parentheses build no AST node** (`parse_primary`'s grouped arm is `return Ok(first)`), so paren nesting spends parse recursion only — which is what makes the split legitimate rather than a loosening. (2) **`MAX_AST_DEPTH` = 16 000 is a DELIBERATE NARROWING of what parses — the first writeup of this row claimed the opposite and was wrong.** The claim was that 16 000 sits above the deepest AST the parser accepted before it existed ("30 nested parens × a 500-fold chain each = 15 000, so nothing that parsed then stops parsing"). That counted only the paren+**infix** shape. A paren level can spend **both** fold loops — `parse_postfix`'s and `parse_bp`'s — for **~998** nodes per level, not 499. **Re-measured 2026-08-13, release binaries, bisected, source `x := ` + *k* × `( … .f×499 +1×499 )`:** `b1307258` accepts *k* = **30** (~**29 940** nodes) and rejects 31; HEAD accepts *k* = **16** (~**15 968** nodes) and rejects 17. So programs between ~16 000 and ~30 000 nodes deep that parsed before now get a clean `expression nested too deeply` — machine-generated code is exactly where those live. **Narrowing was the right call, and it makes the change look better, not worse:** against the measured ~33 100-node cliff the old ceiling had **1.11×** headroom — the parser was accepting programs within ~10% of an uncatchable host SIGABRT on a well-typed program, which is the failure this very row exists to prevent — where 16 000 has **2.07×**. A diagnostic is recoverable; an abort is not. (The `lib.rs` estimate `64 × 500 ≈ 32 k` this correction was originally aimed at turns out to have been very nearly RIGHT at ~29 940; the 7 500 figure was the multiplicative fixture's ceiling only, and 15 000 the single-fold paren shape's — both undercounts of the worst shape.) Buying the depth back needs a bigger `vm::VM_STACK_BYTES`, reserved **per M:N pool worker** — not a bigger constant. Pinned by `parser::tests::deep_iterative_chains_error_not_crash`'s double-fold case (which bisects the single-fold and double-fold shapes and asserts the ratio, so the mechanism is measured rather than re-guessed) and `parser::tests::max_ast_depth_floor_holds` (a FIXED fixture, not derived from the constant, so a silent shrink fails a test). (3) **`chezzi ast` was a latent, not live, crash path** — the `{:#?}` walk was unwrapped, but nothing that parsed could reach a depth that overflowed it. **The design the plan specified was UNSOUND and was replaced with evidence.** The plan bounded `self.depth + chain_total` with `chain_total` an *ambient* per-path fold count decremented on loop exit. A fold loop runs AFTER the descent it sits above, so in `f(g(DEEP) + 1 + 1 + …)` the folds are counted only once `DEEP` has popped its own counts — the two never coexist. **Measured on a probe build with `MAX_DEPTH = 512` and an 8 000 chain ceiling: that shape parses at 45 000 nodes and `chezzi run` dies with `has overflowed its stack`, rc = -6** — the exact crash the gap exists to prevent. The shipped bound is on a **synthesized** measure instead: `Parser::fold_depth` carries the fold depth of the deepest subtree already built, each fold loop resets it before its children and restores `outer.max(self.fold_depth + chain)` on exit, so it `max`-combines over siblings (breadth still costs nothing — a 48 000-element list and a 48 000-argument call both parse) and *sums* up the spine (composition is caught). All **three** backtrack sites that save/restore `self.depth` restore `fold_depth` too, found by grepping `self.pos = save` rather than trusting the list. **The margin is now measured, not derived**: debug `chezzi run` survives 33 000 AST nodes and aborts at 33 500 (the derived 33 100 was right), so against the ~33 100-node derived cliff 16 000 is a **2.07×** margin (33 100/16 000 = 2.06875) and the worst accepted AST (~15 968) is also **2.07×** (33 100/15 968 = 2.0729) — same denominator, two numerators that round the same; against the measured 33 000-node cliff they diverge instead — 16 000 is **2.06×** (33 000/16 000 = 2.0625) and ~15 968 is **2.07×** (33 000/15 968 = 2.0666). **Two asserting tests, Rust by necessity** (a host `SIGABRT`, a chosen thread stack size and a parse-boundary bisection are outside what `tests/chz/` `assert` can express): `parser::tests::max_depth_boundary_accepts_then_rejects` **bisects** the boundary for all seven guarded productions and fails with the measured number (the sizing oracle — a hard-coded boundary rots into prose the moment a parser frame changes width), and `check_errors_json::worst_accepted_nesting_never_signal_crashes` drives `ast`/`check`/`run` over the worst program the parser accepts (the margin oracle — the only test that exercises both constants, both big stacks and the `cmd_ast` hop together, in the debug profile that binds). **(4) SAME-DAY FOLLOW-UP — the bound was PER-`Parser`, so it was a bound on nothing.** Every number above was enforced by the `Parser` object that builds a tree, and an interpolated `{…}` fragment is built by a **second** one: `interpolation::parse_expr_str` re-lexes the fragment text and calls `parser::parse_expr`, whose `depth`/`fold_depth` start at zero. The budgets therefore **composed**. **Measured on `e1137096`**, fixture = *L* levels of `"{ <15 985 deep> }".len()` (three DISTINCT quote styles — an alternating two-quote scheme fails to lex at level 3; every value `int` and type-clean, because a type error short-circuits before the walk that aborts; the `k` paren wraps OUTSIDE the literal, so the enclosing parser and the fragment parser each spend their own budget; `NF + K*499 = 15 485`, just under the cap, so only composition can reject): peak `desugar::walk_expr` depth **15 000 / 30 000 / 45 000** for L = 1/2/3 — exactly the sum. `chezzi check` accepted L = 3 **and** L = 4 (rc 0, ~46 000 and ~62 000 nodes against the ~33 100 cliff — it runs on the 1 GiB front-end stack) while debug `chezzi run` died **rc 134, `thread '<unknown>' has overflowed its stack / fatal runtime error: stack overflow, aborting`** on the 384 MiB VM worker: the uncatchable SIGABRT on a well-typed program this row exists to prevent, one enforcement point short. **The fix bounds the finished tree, not the re-parse.** The plan was to thread a remaining-budget parameter through `parse_expr_str` and have all three `parse_interpolation` consumers pass `MAX_AST_DEPTH − <own walk depth>`; measurement made that unnecessary. `desugar::Walker::walk_expr`'s `ExprKind::Str` arm parses the fragment and **re-enters its own walk on the fragment's subtree**, so a single `Walker` descends the whole composed tree — its recursion depth IS the real depth of what the checker and compiler descend next, exact rather than a per-parse estimate, one number no caller can forget to pass, and its own recursion is bounded by the same check. Every front-end path routes through it (`resolver::build_graph` ends in `desugar::run`; `chezzi run` re-does `build_graph` on the VM thread *before* `compile_graph`; `chezzi check`/`test` and the LSP all go through `build_graph`). The other three `parse_interpolation` callers — `checker::check_interpolation`, `checker::scan_expr_for_pin`, `compiler::compile_str`/`interp_exprs` — are fallbacks for an `ExprKind::Str` this walk did not convert. That is now ONLY a malformed interpolation, which re-fails at the same `parse_interpolation` and descends nothing, so none of them needs a budget of its own; the one other way in — a well-formed literal spliced by a pass-2 default this walk never reached — is the residual described below, closed by W7-51. **No over-narrowing among single-parser shapes, bisected before AND after on release binaries:** double-fold *k* = **16** (~15 968 nodes), flat fold chain **15 997**, postfix chain **15 996**, composed `f(g(…)+1×99)` **127**, parens **254** — all five unchanged, because the worst single-parse tree (15 998 nodes) is under the cap by construction. That is not "nothing that parsed before stops parsing" in general: a SIXTH shape, a default argument spliced by `normalize_call`, composes with the caller's expression exactly like an interpolated fragment and is a program the five-shape bisection never covered. Measured, release binary: `fn f(a: int = <15 000-deep chain>) -> int: return a` called as `x := f() + <2000-deep chain>` — `check` accepted it (rc 0) and `run` printed the answer on `e1137096`; HEAD refuses it with `expression nested too deeply`. What narrowed is composition — both the interpolation seam and this default-splice seam: L ≥ 2 now gets `resolve error (line 1, col 39): expression nested too deeply (limit 16000); this counts the whole expression after desugaring, and an interpolated `{…}` fragment or a spliced default argument nests INSIDE the expression around it and spends the same budget` — the enclosing context is named, because a bare `too deeply` pointing inside a fragment tells the user nothing about who ate the budget. Statement nesting cannot compose (a fragment holds an expression, never a block) and stays bounded by `MAX_DEPTH`. Pinned by `check_errors_json::composed_interp_depth_is_bounded_globally`, **Rust by necessity and verified to fail pre-fix in both profiles** (`code() == None` in debug, a missing diagnostic in release): a host SIGABRT is invisible from inside the program and a compile-time refusal is outside what `recover:` can catch, so `tests/chz/` cannot express either. **Two residual test holes closed with it.** `parser::tests::max_ast_depth_floor_holds` pinned a **15 000**-fold fixture against a real flat-chain ceiling of 15 997, so `MAX_AST_DEPTH` could have been silently cut to 15 100 with every depth test green — widened to 15 950. And `deep_iterative_chains_error_not_crash`'s "the assertion is a RATIO, so it survives any future value of `MAX_AST_DEPTH`" was false above ~50 800: each `(…)` level costs ~2 parse frames, so the single-fold bisection saturates against `MAX_DEPTH` at ~254 levels and at `MAX_AST_DEPTH = 60_000` the test goes red blaming a fold loop when the CONSTANT is what moved — it now asserts the non-saturation precondition (`MAX_AST_DEPTH / N < MAX_DEPTH / 2`) first, with a message that says which knob to turn (a `const` block, so it is a BUILD error past ~51 200, not a red run). **One residual was OPEN when this row first shipped, and is now CLOSED by W7-51 (2026-08-13) — which is why the claim above is unqualified.** Found by adversarial review of this very fix: `desugar`'s `regs` was built ONCE before both passes, so a spliced default was raw declaration AST, and `normalize_call` spliced it in the **tail** of `walk_expr_inner` — after that node's children were walked. A splice performed on **pass 2** was therefore never walked, and there was no pass 3; a default-of-a-default is exactly that shape. A well-formed interpolated literal inside one survived as `ExprKind::Str` and was re-parsed by `checker::check_interpolation` / `compiler::compile_str` with a fresh `Parser`. **Measured then**, `fn g(a: int = "{ 1+1×15990 }".len())` / `fn h(b: int = g())` / `x := h()+1×15990`: `check` rc 0, the guard's counter peaked at **15 995**, the tree the checker and compiler descend was **~31 986** nodes — a **1.03×** margin against the measured ~33 100 cliff, i.e. worse than the 1.11× correction (2) above condemns. Latent, not live, and pre-existing (accepted on `e1137096` too). **W7-51 removed both of its preconditions** — the driver is ONE pass, and a non-literal default is no longer spliced as an expression at all (it is compiled once in its defining module; the call site gets a two-node call to a provider whose body is walked as an ordinary top-level `fn`, at depth 0), while the literal class that IS still cloned both excludes any `Str` carrying `{`/`}` and is re-walked by `splice_default`. So `Walker::depth` is now an upper bound on the tree the checker and compiler descend — which is what this bound was for. **Re-verified independently on `ed4830b3`, 2026-08-13**, with a probe on `checker::check_interpolation`'s success arm (it fires exactly when a well-formed `Str` reached the checker un-converted), both binaries identical but for the probe, on the fixture above: **`925dd0f7` 1 hit / peak walk depth 15 995; `ed4830b3` 0 hits / peak 15 994**; `chezzi run` prints `15995` rc 0 on both, and on `--serial` too. **Negative control, same `ed4830b3` build with the desugar `Str → Interp` conversion disabled: 2 hits** — so the zero is a live zero, not a dead probe. `check_errors_json::composed_interp_depth_is_bounded_globally` arm (c) is retained as defence in depth: accepted or refused, it must never signal-crash. **Method lesson, now in `docs/contributing/bug-discovery.md`:** when you enforce a bound on an artifact, enumerate every writer that can still mutate it after the check, not just every reader — a tail-position mutation inside the same walk is the easiest to miss, because it does not look like a second pipeline stage. **POST-REVIEW FIX WAVE (2026-08-13) — a THIRD iterative fold loop was counted by neither depth counter, and it is pre-existing.** `parse_type_postfix` (`src/parser/mod.rs`) is a `loop` that wraps a `Type::Generic` per `?`/`!` — a left-leaning TYPE tree the front-end walkers descend — and it bumped neither `self.depth` nor `fold_depth`, so it is the same class as the two expression fold loops this row fixed and the one shape the per-production sizing oracle skips. **Measured: `fn f(x: int` + `?!` × 350 000 + `) -> int:` → `fatal runtime error: stack overflow, aborting` on `b1307258` AND on `dfdc7a1b`; 250 000 was accepted.** (The recursive sibling forms were already guarded — `List[` × 300 000 and `[` × 300 000 both refuse cleanly — so this was the last loop of its class.) It is now charged the same way `parse_postfix`'s loop is: `parse_type` opens the fold scope (reset before the children, `max`-combined on the way out, so sibling type arguments are breadth and cost the deepest one, not their sum) and `parse_type_postfix` sums its chain on top. A type postfix chain past `MAX_AST_DEPTH` is now a clean `type nested too deeply`. Pinned by three new cases in `parser::tests::deep_iterative_chains_error_not_crash` (over-cap refusal, under-cap acceptance, and a signature far wider than the cap), verified to fail with the guard removed. Note the fixture alternates `?!`: the lexer folds `??` into the null-coalescing token | — |
